#2806. Patriots designate starting guard Alijah Vera-Tucker to return from injured reserve

As the New England Patriots headed back to work following the bye week, so did Alijah Vera-Tucker.

The starting left guard was present for Monday's bonus practice inside the WIN Waste Innovations Field House. His 21-day activation window, in turn, has been opened at the earliest date that it could be.

Vera Tucker, 27, sustained a hamstring injury in October and was placed on injured reserve in the hours leading up to the team's 30-24 loss to the Chicago Bears. The 6-foot-4, 308-pound offseason signing is now eligible to return to the 53-man roster in time for the final matchup of November after missing the NFL minimum of four games.

In the interim, New England's interior offensive line depth includes Greg Van Roten, Matt Pryor, Walter Rouse and a previously scratched Ben Brown around starting center Jared Wilson. And with backup Tommy DeVito next up at quarterback in the fallout of captain Drake Maye's fractured fibula.

"I think AVT's got a chance to be out there," Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel told reporters ahead of Monday's practice, while also noting that wide receiver Romeo Doubs' return from a shoulder injury could be near. "I think Romeo, maybe later in the week. Too soon to tell right now."

Arriving on a three-year contract as an unrestricted free agent in March, Vera-Tucker stands six starts into his stay in Foxborough. He had played 100 percent of the offensive snaps before departing in the second quarter of what became a 26-19 win over his former New York Jets.

The USC product entered the NFL in the first round of the 2021 draft at No. 14 overall. Despite prior stints on injured reserve in 2022, 2023 and 2025, he stands 49 starts and 2,985 offensive downs into his AFC East career.

League rules permit eight return designations from injured reserve over the course of a regular season. Six of those eight have now been utilized by last year's conference champions.

The Los Angeles Chargers host next Sunday's flexed-out 4:05 p.m. ET kickoff at SoFi Stadium.

#2805. ‘Worst COP ever’: Climate talks collapse after draft deal fails to include fossil fuels

Negotiations at the United Nations' climate summit collapsed on Sunday as the U.K., EU and others rejected a draft deal that failed to include fossil fuels.

Since the U.N.'s Climate Change Conference — or COP31 — began in Antalya, Turkey, on Nov. 9, almost 200 countries have been locked in talks to reach a "cover decision," with a number of drafts issued in recent days.

Antonio Guterres, the U.N.'s secretary-general, said he was "deeply disappointed" that no consensus was reached.

Talks centered on electrification, funding for developing nations and ways to respond to scientific warnings about rising global temperatures, with the 1.5 degrees Celsius target likely to be overshot.

But on Sunday, after negotiations overran by almost two days, the latest cover proposal made no mention of fossil fuels, prompting outcry from governments and campaigners.

Speaking on behalf of the EU, Ireland's climate minister Darragh O'Brien said the conference was "hijacked by some oil states," when asked by Reuters about Saudi Arabia's role in the negotiations. CNBC has contacted Saudi Arabia for comment.

The EU said it rejected the deal because the text "does not respond to the science" of climate change. "We will not accept a COP that greenwashes fossil fuels," it said in a statement Sunday.

The U.K. government "does not believe the cover decision proposed by the Presidencies meets the moment," it said on Sunday. "It moves us backwards on the science when we must accelerate implementation," it said.

Representatives of island nations — vulnerable to the effects of climate change — refused to sign the draft text. "Three years ago in Dubai, the nations of the world agreed by consensus to transition away from fossil fuels in their energy systems," Philip Davis, prime minister of the Bahamas, said in an online statement, referring to the UAE Consensus made at COP28 in 2023.

"Small island states produce less than 1% of global emissions, yet we carry the weight of a crisis we did not cause. We have brought plans, commitments and patience to three decades of these talks. Antalya exhausted that patience," Davis said.

Albert Salamanca, a senior research fellow at the Stockholm Environment Institute Asia, told CNBC via email that the lack of consensus on a deal is "tantamount to a death sentence for vulnerable communities still unable to respond in a timely manner to emerging risks."

Developing nations are facing "abandonment," according to Mohamed Adow, director of climate and energy think tank Power Shift Africa. He described the event as "the worst COP ever" in an online statement, adding that "rich countries blocked the creation of a just transition mechanism."

The World Wildlife Fund, or WWF, described the lack of progress on finance for developing nations as having "real-world consequences for the lives and livelihoods of the planet's most vulnerable communities," in an online statement.

Nonsikelelo Nkomo-Mabobe, business development manager at Solidaridad, an organization that promotes fair supply chains, welcomed some of the language in COP31's proposed Antalya Pact, with "promising signs around food security, which is acknowledged as heavily affected by climate impacts." But she said a finance gap remains.

"Funding must be easier for frontline communities to access, and it must be traceable once it has been disbursed," Nkomo-Mabobe said in an email to CNBC.

The WWF welcomed the engagement from participants of the COP31 Action Agenda, which comprises of businesses and other non-governmental organizations. "Momentum from communities, cities and businesses is real despite what happened in the negotiating rooms," it said.

A major goal of COP31 was to increase global electrification to 35% by 2035 — up from 23% today, with a Global Climate Action Agenda Electrification Delivery Package announced on Thursday as a "first milestone" to achieve this.

The U.N.'s Climate Change Executive Secretary Simon Stiell said on Sunday that "around half" the global economy is accelerating electrification, adding that the package had made "real economy" progress. More than 90 countries signed the pledge, according to Australia's Climate Change and Energy Minister Chris Bowen, COP31's president of negotiations.

"Frustratingly," the pledge does not detail how the electricity will be generated, according to Ellen Dorsey, executive director of the Wallace Global Fund — around 57% of electricity was generated by fossil fuels in 2025, according to think tank Ember.

Dorsey urged states to provide electricity to those in developing countries. "Will we ensure governments provide electricity to hundreds of millions who lack it, by leapfrogging fossil fuels build-out and instead invest in small-scale renewables?" she said in an email to CNBC.

Preserving sea life has "often been overlooked" in climate negotiations, according to the U.N. The language referring to oceans in the draft cover decision was "slightly weakened," according to Nicolas Entrup, director of international relations at marine conservation organization OceanCare, in an email to CNBC. But he said he was hopeful that the Antalya Ocean Current, a U.N. plan to hold future talks on oceans and climate change, would be carried forward.

More than 95% of island and coastal nations now have ocean preservation in their climate plans, but around 60% of those commitments rely on external funding, according to research by ocean conservancy groups. Scientists also called for ongoing investment to monitor oceans beyond short-term research.

"I share the deep disappointment of many: COP31 has been unable to agree on issues related to both Presidency's led decisions," Stiell said. Turkey and Australia shared the presidency at this year's conference.

- CNBC's Sawdah Bhaimiya contributed to this report.

Clarification: This story has been updated to more accurately reflect Nicolas Entrup's comments.

#2803. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2801. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

#2799. 2026-27 NBA News, Injury Updates Nov. 23: Dylan Harper, CJ McCollum out, but Brandon Ingram returning

Welcome to our weekly Monday Morning NBA notebook, a quick hit of NBA news and injuries from around the league to catch you up on what happened in the past week. While a couple more big names go on the injury list, we get at least one All-Star off that list.

While nobody is saying this is a good thing, it's also a lot better outcome than was feared when it happened. Spurs second-year guard Dylan Harper suffered a left hip external rotator strain when he slipped on a wet spot on the floor. Officially, he is out 1-3 weeks, but the smart bet is on a couple of weeks.

Harper has become the Spurs' second-best player, already averaging 18.4 points on 50.5% shooting, plus 5.4 rebounds and 4.5 assists per game, while playing high-level defense. His absence means a lot more Stephon Castle with the ball in his hands — which is not a bad thing, but his handle is a little more loose, and he had seven turnovers in a sloppy win against the Clippers.

• Guard CJ McCollum suffered a strained left calf and will be reevaluated in four weeks, the team announced.

• Big man Asa Newell has surgery to repair a left great toe hyperextension, and while the Hawks' announcement says he will be re-evaluated in four months, that will be mid-March, weeks away from the end of the regular season (April 11). It's unlikely we see Newell again this season.

McCollum had moved to the bench already to make way for more minutes for rookie point guard Kingston Flemings, and the offense plays faster and looks better to the eye test with him in there.

There is one NBA All-Star getting healthy and returning to play.

When Ingram underwent heel surgery last May, doctors discovered a partially torn Achilles tendon. To be clear, this wasn't a full-blown rupture, but he was going to miss time, and the Clippers were aware of it when they traded for him as part of the Kawhi Leonard deal.

Ingram is coming off a bounce-back season in Toronto, averaging 21.5 points a game, shooting 38.2% from 3, grabbing 5.6 rebounds a night, and earning an All-Star selection. The Clippers could use that kind of production to lift their 23rd-ranked offense.

• Tyrese Haliburton is still at least a couple of weeks away from a return from a hamstring injury. He said on social media his goal is to be back before the end of November. The Pacers are 3-8 without him.

• The Grizzlies GG Jackson underwent surgery Tuesday to repair a broken metatarsal in his left foot, and while the team says he will be re-evaluated in three months, expect him to miss most of the rest of the season. Which sucks. Jackson broke the same bone in his right foot a couple of years ago.

• Sacramento signed veteran point guard Gabe Vincent to back up Darius Acuff Jr. It's a homecoming for Vincent, who was born and raised in central California.

• Kawhi Leonard remains out in Toronto but should return in the "next few weeks," reports ESPN's Shams Charania. History suggests taking any news of a Leonard return with a grain of salt.

• Chicago's Norman Powell tweaked his hamstring and will miss a game or two, he will be re-evaluated later in the week.

• There is a lot of manufactured buzz about the Dejounte Murray trade, because that's good for traffic, but the reality is there is no traction right now around a deal. Not to the Suns, not to anyone. Murray missed the majority of a couple of seasons with a torn Achilles, hasn't looked the same, he's not shooting well from 3 or playing like the All-Star player he was five years ago, and has a player option for $30 million next season. Teams view him as a negative contract and want a first-round pick or a pick swap attached to have a discussion. New Orleans isn't going there, at least not yet. Expect this to drag out until the new year and likely close to the February trade deadline.

• The sense in league circles is that Washington coach Brian Keefe's seat is getting hot. However, Doug Christie's seat in Sacramento is not.

#2798. Wall Street's 'Fear Gauge' just flashed a buy sign

Last week's sell-off could be a dip-buyer's dream, if history repeats itself.As the Nasdaq-100 Index touched bear market territory at Friday's low, and the S&P 500 slipped to 12% off its highs, the Cboe Volatility Index (VIX) reached above 40, the highest since April 2025 and in the top 5% of readings for the so-called "fear gauge" going back to 1990, according to Charlie Bilello, chief market strategist at Creative Planning.Good news for bulls: S&P 500 returns look well above-average following such days. The one-year return after fifth-percentile VIX readings is 29%, more than double the 12% from all other VIX levels, Bilello writes. Two years later, the return is 46%, compared to 25% normally.A similar analysis by Noel Smith of Kai Asset Management shows that of the 13 times VIX has touched 40 since 1990, the median 1-month return of the S&P 500 is 3.6%, and the 3-month return 8.1%, compared to 1.2% and 3.2%, respectively, during all other times.

The sell-off was also unique in its timing on the calendar, with November traditionally the best month for stocks, data from the Stock Trader's Almanac show.The S&P's 5.6% drop from Wednesday's close through Friday was the worst Thursday/Friday combo in a week before the Thanksgiving holiday since the index started trading in 1957, according to OddStats on X.It was also only the fourth time in the history of the Invesco QQQ Trust that the fund was down at least 2% on a Thursday and 5% on Friday. The other instances were during Liberation Week last year, and two in the early dot-com bust. One of those periods is more encouraging than the other, but both led to a median 7.5% rally the next week, according to OddStats.

#2792. Nets vs. Timberwolves Preview: Julius Randle returns to Minnesota

MINNEAPOLIS, MN – NOVEMBER 29: Julius Randle #30 of the Minnesota Timberwolves signs autographs for fans before the game against the LA Clippers during the Emirates NBA Cup game on November 29, 2024 at Target Center in Minneapolis, Minnesota. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this Photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2024 NBAE (Photo by David Sherman/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

The Brooklyn Nets followed up their embarrassing outing against the Chicago Bulls with a spirited effort against the Orlando Magic. Brooklyn had their chances late, but couldn't cash in. On the other side, Franz Wagner continued his All-Star level play and hit the game winner in the final seconds of the game. Altogether, it was the Nets' fourth straight loss.

The opponent tonight has managed some rocky moments, but are doing well despite them. The Minnesota Timberwolves are near the top of the jam-packed Western Conference and figure to be a formidable opponent in the playoffs. They helped their cause with a great 104-95 win against the Memphis Grizzlies on Saturday night.

Matchup: Brooklyn Nets (3-12) at Minnesota Timberwolves (12-5)

Location: Target Center (Minneapolis, MN)

OUT – Nolan Traore (right hamstring strain)

OUT – Jaylen Clark (right hamstring strain)

OUT – Donte DiVincenzo (right Achilles tendon repair)

Full NBA injury report for November 23 here.

Location matters a great deal. When you're in one environment, the things you normally do are frowned upon and minimized. But when you move, what was mocked becomes celebrated. That's the story of LaMelo Ball's run so far. He was loathed by many an NBA observer while with the Charlotte Hornets. But now that he's on a winning team, he's gotten a lot more praise and credit for his play. Even with that, there's still room for the young guard to grow.

"The key here, like with the whole offense, is LaMelo Ball. He's had some wild swings between offensive genius and being in way over his head during his first month running Chris Finch's offense.

It often looks like LaMelo is trying to play free jazz while McDaniels, Dosunmu, Kuminga, and Gobert are much more popular with the standard pop hits. It's clunky, but the Wolves are no worse for wear. The bug in the system hasn't affected their clutch-time offense, which ranks fifth in the NBA, and 66.7% of their clutch field goals are assisted.

The Wolves look like contenders a month into the season. All teams have some kinks to work out. The ball movement can always be better; just ask Gregg Popovich and Steve Kerr. But Minnesota's iso heavy offense is still strong enough to go deep into the playoffs, especially when Anthony Edwards returns to the lineup with a vengeance."

That's one of those things that'll get settled the more experience everyone has with each other. By the time the Nets see the Wolves in February, they should be a lot better on offense.

The Wolves have arguably the best defense in the NBA to start the season, and a lot of their success is due to their ability to limit three-pointers. Minnesota is only allowing 35 three-point attempts per game, fifth-fewest in the league. Even more impressively, they're holding teams to 32.8% from downtown, third best in the league. It's hard to get shots up against this team, and without Traore, scoring will be a bit harder for the Nets to come by tonight. Look for Egor Demin to return to the starting five and for Mikel Brown Jr to get all the minutes he can handle.

Brooklyn will need a big game out of Michael Porter Jr tonight. Porter Jr can heat up at a moment's notice and that skill will come in handy for Brooklyn tonight. Brooklyn needs wins in the worst way and if the defense can hold up long enough to keep it close late, maybe Porter Jr can make magic happen at the end.

A homecoming! Randle spent two seasons in Minnesota, helping the Timberwolves reach the Western Conference finals during his time there. He figures to receive a nice hand from the Target Center faithful this evening.

On the court, Randle will look to bounce back from a rough ending to Friday's game. The Nets had their chances to win late, but a Randle turnover and missed three -pointer at the buzzer doomed Brooklyn's chances of pulling off the upset. Randle should be amped up and with a chance to end the team's losing streak, he'll have even more motivation to play a great game.

Randle will be matched up with an old teammate of his. Jaden McDaniels has improved each season of his career and earned himself a nice extension for his efforts. As one of the more versatile forwards in basketball, McDaniels can take on a variety of roles and impacts the game in multiple ways. With Randle almost certainly looking to hunt for his shot tonight, McDaniels will play a bigger role than usual for the Wolves.

#2787. Here’s how to watch Bengals vs. Commanders for free: Time, livestream

The Cincinnati Bengals continue their manifest destiny march toward the 2027 Super Bowl tonight.

At 8:15 p.m. ET, Joe Burrow's 7-2 squad visits Jayden Daniels' Washington Commanders for a huge Week 11 Monday Night Football matchup at Northwest Stadium.

Ahead of this evening's showdown, Cincy is riding a five-game winning streak behind Burrow's MVP-like play, cobbling together impressive victories over the Dolphins, Ravens, Titans, Falcons and Steelers.

Washington's season hasn't been as straightforward. Currently 3-5-1, the team has scored gutsy wins over the Seahawks, Giants and Eagles along with a few rough losses and that pesky tie (also to the Giants).

Tonight, oddsmakers give the Bengals a six-point edge over the Commanders… but Dan Quinn's club could play spoiler.

"What I think it's really gonna come down to is 'do you have the right leverage,'" Quinn noted. "Can you tackle well. And inside the pocket that's the other part when the second play begins."

Also, of note, both starting quarterbacks tonight — Burrow and Daniels — are Heisman Trophy-winning LSU alumni.

Don't miss out. This could be a barnburner.

#2785. Zelenskyy praises ‘exemplary’ drone operation that sank Russian warship in a Libyan port

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy on Monday lauded an "exemplary" military operation that sank one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port, after Kyiv's forces struck their target with several domestically made naval drones.

The incident followed a suspected Ukrainian drone strike on a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea on Saturday and occurred less than a week after damage was inflicted on Russian President Vladimir Putin's prized Crimea bridge.

In a social media post, Zelenskyy said Ukrainian forces deployed seven sea drones to attack the Merkury, a Russian navy corvette, at Benghazi's port in the early hours of Sunday morning. Ukraine's president posted dramatic video footage showing Ukraine's naval drones triggering a massive explosion on the Russian ship.

"Despite all the enemy's efforts to counter the attack, the Russian corvette Merkury was sunk. It's one of their newest ships, commissioned in 2023, during the full-scale war," Zelenskyy said. "We continue our operations in designated waters," he added.

Satellite imagery taken on Sunday showed the Merkury mostly underwater, having capsized

The Russian dry cargo ship MV Angara, which Zelenskyy said Moscow used to transport weapons, was hit by Ukrainian forces. A Ka-27 military helicopter on the Merkury was also destroyed.

Asked about reports of attacks on Russian ships off the coast of Libya, Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov said information on this would be provided via the Defense Ministry.

The Security Service of Ukraine, the SBU, said Monday that the attacks "once again demonstrated that the Russian military fleet and its logistics have no safe routes even far beyond the Black Sea," according to a Google translation.

"Such unique special operations destroy the enemy's logistical capabilities and force Russia to pay for aggression even at a strategic distance from Ukraine," the SBU said via Telegram.

The United Nations Support Mission in Libya, or UNSMIL, expressed "serious concern" over the drone attacks in a statement saying the country "cannot afford, and must not become a battleground for foreign conflicts."

"UNSMIL calls for an immediate, independent and transparent investigation to establish the facts and hold those responsible to account," it added.

The incident is not the first time Libya has seen attacks linked to the Russia-Ukraine war near its territory.

In March, Moscow's Transport Ministry accused Ukraine of striking the Arctic Metagaz, a liquefied natural gas tanker, as part of a suspected sea drone attack in the Mediterranean between Libya and Malta.

#2784. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2783. Why Brandon Williams' return should boost the Warriors' struggling offense

Why Brandon Williams' return should boost the Warriors' struggling offense originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – After five consecutive games in which the Warriors' offense too often slowed to a drip that kept them well below typical NBA scoring, help is on the way.

It's not coming from Stephen Curry. Not yet.

But Brandon Williams, the quickest man on the roster, is expected to return Wednesday night after missing six games with a mild left hamstring strain. His presence and ability to attack almost any defense immediately intensifies the offense.

"Ball pressure really hurt us, kind of put us on our heels," coach Steve Kerr said Monday, referring Golden State's recent 0-5 road trip. "You can't do that with Brandon. He'll just blow right by you. He'll be a big help, and him going out at the same time as Steph was a big blow and another reason for kind of this offensive disruption that we've had."

After averaging 91.8 points over the last six games, maybe now Golden State can remind scoreboards around the league that Stephen Curry's absence did not spur any intent to recreate the low-scoring offenses so prevalent in the 1990s.

(Example: Only three teams – the Bulls, Jazz and Suns – averaged more than 101 points in 1996-97).

This season, the Hornets (99.9 points per game) are the only team averaging fewer than 106 points. Eleven teams average more than 115 points.

Coming off a miserable road trip, the Warriors returned to Chase Center on Sunday and were able to grind out an 88-87 victory over Boston. The win was a tonic, but the offensive output, including 40-percent shooting from the field, including 31 percent from distance, is not a sustainable winning formula in today's NBA.

"No matter what the score is," Kerr said, "it's fun to see when a group that's struggling really summons a great effort collectively and fights and competes and plays through all the mistakes and tough calls and flurries everything else. I took a lot of joy in that game to see how our guys responded from what was a tough trip."

Yet the Warriors know they need to bump up their scoring. Even during their 88-point game against the Celtics, there were signs of improvement.

Charles Bassey made his third consecutive start, producing 13 points and nine rebounds; he will remain in the starting lineup. Inserting Bassey sends Draymond Green to power forward, where his proficient shooting from the 3-point corners – he made three triples in the first six minutes against Boston – are another source of scoring.

In addition, Kerr has indicated rookie forward Yaxel Lendeborg will spend more time posting up. As he should; he leads the league points per possession on post ups.

Williams, however, brings a missing dimension. He's Golden State's fourth-leading scorer, averaging 10.4 points per game. He provides a boost off the bench if Curry is healthy and is a crucial element with him on the sidelines.

He's the only player on the active roster who can consistently blast through traffic in the paint and find success.

#2777. ‘We’re defending’ | Improved defense sparks Wizards’ three-game winning streak

The Washington Wizards claimed defense as their identity entering the 2026-27 season. Gone were the days of the bottom-five defensive rating they'd owned each of the previous three seasons.

That proved true during a 4-3 start where their defensive rating ranked seventh in the NBA. But that figure dropped to 26th amid an eight-game losing streak in which they allowed 129.5 points per game. Their defensive effort had waned, and so, too, had their production.

Then a players-only meeting was held. The main point: The Wizards needed to play with more physicality, to show more fight. They responded with a three-game winning streak in which they held their opponent under 100 points in three straight contests for the first time since Nov. 13, 2021.

"We're defending. I think that's the biggest thing for us," Brian Keefe said. "You obviously want to make shots like we did tonight, but you've got to hang your hat on something. Got to hang your hat on defense … We're not where we want to be. We can still get better in that, but those are the consistent habits that we want to establish for this team."

The Wizards started their winning streak with a 103-99 win over the Toronto Raptors, followed by a 98-94 victory over the Chicago Bulls. Both contests featured improved defensive intensity for a group that lacked it over its previous eight games.

Key in those wins was Bilal Coulibaly's defense. The fourth-year wing held Norman Powell to three points in the second half of Friday's win over Chicago, including a fourth-quarter sequence in which he blocked the 2026 All-Star twice in one possession.

Two nights later, Coulibaly helped hold Franz Wagner to just 18 points on 7-for-18 shooting. His defense is already elite. But he's striving for even more production.

"I always take a matchup personal. I really want to be one of the best defensive player in the league. I think I'm showing that I'm getting there."

The Wizards have jumped from 26th to 20th in defensive rating since that win over Toronto. They remain bottom-six in offensive efficiency and bottom-five in offensive rating, yet they're playing their best basketball of the season.

After Washington's 136-120 loss to the Memphis Grizzlies, Kyshawn George said the team lacked accountability. That was clear by the team's play and the coaching staff's actions. The same players were playing 20 minutes a night despite poor effort, limited offensive production and nonexistent defense.

Keefe has since altered those rotations. Bub Carrington has played just 11 minutes over the past four games — most of which came at the end of blowouts. Tre Johnson didn't play in the second half against Toronto and Chicago. Deandre Ayton played just five minutes against Orlando.

Keefe has also put the ball in AJ Dybantsa's hands, which has unlocked the No. 1 pick's downhill scoring ability. Dybantsa is averaging 19.7 points and 10.0 rebounds on 48.9% shooting over those three games.

At 7-11, the Wizards are ninth in the Eastern Conference. They're finally holding each other accountable. And it's producing wins.

"We have high character players. We're not here to just go through the motions and just be average," George said. "We want to accomplish something. We're playing to establish ourselves as not only players but also an organization. When the whole team has the same feeling, it's on us to just go out there and do our job. I think that's what we've been doing past the games."

#2774. Netflix’s ‘The Altruists’ sparks FTX portrayal debate among crypto bigwigs from CZ to Star Xu

Netflix's "The Altruists," released Nov. 19, has sparked backlash from prominent cryptocurrency industry figures, including Binance co-founder Changpeng Zhao, over its portrayal of the collapse of FTX.

Critics have questioned the accuracy of the series' portrayal of the FTX collapse, particularly how it assigns responsibility of the scandal. Several crypto industry figures argue that it softens the culpability of Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison while casting Zhao — widely known as CZ — as an outsized villain.

Zhao criticized the series in a post on X, calling it "insane" and questioning whether the show was intended to influence public perception. He declined to comment to CNBC beyond his social-media posts.

Zhao pleaded guilty in 2023 to failing to maintain an effective anti-money laundering program at Binance, in violation of the U.S. Bank Secrecy Act, in a case separate from the FTX fraud. He was sentenced to four months in prison in 2024 and pardoned by President Donald Trump in October 2025.

FTX filed for bankruptcy in November 2022 after a liquidity crisis exposed deep financial problems at the exchange. Investigations later found that billions of dollars in customer funds had been diverted to Alameda Research.

Shortly before FTX's collapse, Zhao announced that Binance would liquidate its remaining holdings of FTT, FTX's native token, intensifying pressure on the exchange.

Bankman-Fried was sentenced to 25 years in prison in March 2024 after being convicted of fraud and conspiracy charges. Ellison pleaded guilty to fraud and conspiracy, and later cooperated with prosecutors, according to the Justice Department.

Crypto commentator Kaleo wrote on X that the series portrayed Bankman-Fried and Ellison as having been pressured and groomed by Zhao into committing their alleged crimes, while casting Zhao as the figure who orchestrated the scandal.

"Whoever wrote this script is shameless," Kaleo added. He identifies himself as a co-founder of NFT project LedgArt.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin said in a reply on X that the show exaggerated Zhao's accent to the point of raising questions about whether the portrayal was racist.

Meanwhile, Tigran Gambaryan, a former Binance executive and U.S. federal agent, commented on LinkedIn that the series downplayed Bankman-Fried's responsibility while portraying Zhao as a "cartoonish villain," undermining the showrunners' credibility.

Binance co-founder Yi He criticized the series for portraying its characters as "one-dimensional" and "stereotyped," while suggesting that it shifted blame away from those responsible for FTX's collapse.

She compared the show unfavorably with Chinese short-form drama platform Hongguo, saying Netflix "might as well be canceled."

But the reactions were not uniformly supportive of Zhao.

In a post on X, OKX founder Star Xu said that rather than dismissing criticism as coordinated attacks by competitors, Zhao should respond with facts and evidence.

Xu was responding to a post questioning whether accounts defending Zhao were part of a bot network.

The eight-episode drama stars Anthony Boyle as FTX founder Sam Bankman-Fried, Julia Garner as former Alameda Research CEO Caroline Ellison and Terry Chen as Zhao.

Netflix did not respond to a request for comment.

In an interview published by Netflix before the series' release, Graham Moore, the show's creator and co-showrunner, said he encouraged the actors to develop fully three-dimensional characters rather than simply imitate the real-life figures they portrayed.

Speaking to Business Insider, Moore said, "I can never prove what was going on in the hearts or minds of Sam's friends when they made those decisions."

Moore said he drew on extensive research into the real-life figures' backgrounds and public statements to imagine what may have driven the fractures between them.

"The story we're telling is not a financial story, and it's not a crypto story. It's a deeply personal story," he added.

While criticism of "The Altruists" has so far unfolded largely on social media, Netflix has previously faced defamation lawsuits over dramatizations of real-life events, including "Inventing Anna" and "Baby Reindeer."

Rachel DeLoache Williams, a former Vanity Fair photo editor, sued Netflix in 2022 over her portrayal in "Inventing Anna," a series about convicted fraudster Anna Sorokin.

Williams alleged that the show used her real name and personal details while falsely depicting her as greedy, dishonest and disloyal, including portraying her as exploiting her friendship with Sorokin for financial gain.

The lawsuit was dismissed in February 2026 after the parties reached a settlement. Netflix and Williams' attorney reportedly confirmed the settlement in separate statements, saying, "Williams and Netflix have resolved the lawsuit."

In 2024, Fiona Harvey, who said she inspired the character Martha in "Baby Reindeer," sued Netflix for at least $170 million. Harvey alleged that the series falsely portrayed her as a convicted stalker who had served time in prison and sexually assaulted the show's creator, Richard Gadd.

The case is reportedly scheduled to go to trial in May 2027.

"We intend to defend this matter vigorously and to stand by Richard Gadd's right to tell his story," Netflix said in a statement reported by the Associated Press. She also challenged the show's claim to be based on a true story and alleged that viewers were able to identify her as the character depicted.

New York Giants starting cornerback Greg Newsome will be out multiple weeks with a high ankle sprain, head coach John Harbaugh said on Monday.

#2767. U.S. probes Megaspeed, Alice Huang as new figure emerges in Nvidia chip case

The U.S. is investigating Alice Huang and her former company, Megaspeed, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said, as a report identified one of her associates as a key decision-maker at the Singapore-based company.

Clayton said the investigation was "ongoing" on Sunday, the day before Nvidia announced it had reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, a position he resigned from in part because of his relationship with Alice Huang.

Alice Huang was a key figure in Megaspeed International, a cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia. U.S. intelligence reportedly believes she is a Chinese spy.

"There is an investigation ongoing into her firm and into her," Clayton told ABC on Sunday, adding that, "Nvidia chips, they are the jewel. They are the absolute top that everybody wants, that's why we're focusing on this."

Nvidia's board announced Jensen Huang's return before the opening bell on Monday, saying it had "a series of discussions and meetings" with him.

It added that it received input from senior levels of the United States government" and is confident that his reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

Alice Huang's associate, Chinese national Li Ming, who is now out on bail in Singapore, was "deeply involved" in Megaspeed's activities and took part in major decisions, The Wall Street Journal reported earlier on Saturday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The Singapore charges against Li concern alleged false representations to Super Micro Computer, a U.S.-based firm with known links to chipmaker Nvidia, though Megaspeed is not named in those charges.

Last week, the Commerce and Justice departments said that the investigation into Megaspeed began in 2024 over potential export-control violations. Their joint statement said Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security and Singapore law enforcement interviewed Huang during an on-site check in June 2025.

The Justice Department later took the lead in the criminal investigation.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CBS on Sunday that the administration was seeking answers from Nvidia about its chip sales to China, including who oversaw compliance and which levels of the company knew about the activity.

Separately, The New York Times reported Sunday that Alice Huang was in contact with an officer of a top Chinese intelligence agency from spring 2025 through fall 2026, citing people briefed on U.S. intelligence reports.

Bloomberg reported last week that the Justice Department is investigating whether entities linked to Alice Huang helped smuggle Super Micro products to China.

Li faces five charges in Singapore, including two counts of fraud by false representation, along with allegations involving the operation of a company he controlled, unauthorized bank-account access and another company director's duties.

Prosecutors allege he falsely told Super Micro that the company would hold and lease the servers it purchased.

The Journal reported that Li is out on bail and that his case is ongoing. He was offered bail of S$1 million in March 2025. The Singapore charges do not name Megaspeed.

Earlier this month, Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said that police had found insufficient evidence to establish that Alice Huang committed offenses related to the possible inaccurate declarations under investigation.

The ministry said she was issued a stern warning for providing information that police assessed as knowingly inaccurate and for downplaying her role at Megaspeed. It also said Singapore had not investigated her for espionage.

Megaspeed has said it is a "legitimate and lawful business" and that Huang left the company in August 2025.

The scrutiny is set to intensify in Washington. Jensen Huang and the company's general counsel, Tim Teter, have agreed to closed-door, transcribed interviews with three House committees on Dec. 1 and Dec. 2, respectively.

The committees said they want answers about Nvidia's role in allowing Chinese actors to access advanced chips and the involvement of a potential Chinese intelligence asset.

Speaking to CNBC's Squawk Box on Sunday U.S. time, Ryan Fedasiuk, a former advisor for U.S.-China Bilateral Affairs at the State Department, said that there was "still a lot we don't know" about the case.

He said lawmakers would likely focus on Nvidia's customer relationships and "who knew what at the company when."

Fedasiuk added that there was "a very good chance" investigators would find that people at Nvidia knew their products could be used for restricted purposes. He characterized the existing evidence as containing "multiple" smoking guns.

#2761. Trump hosts crypto gala as his meme coin falls below $1

U.S. President Donald Trump on Sunday hosted his third cryptocurrency gala for top holders of his meme coin, $TRUMP, which has plummeted to below $1 a coin after reaching a high of $75 the day before Trump's second inauguration, on Jan. 19, 2025.

The dinner, held at the Trump National golf club in Sterling, Virginia, comes as some Democrats say they will investigate potential conflicts of interest and accuse Trump of using the presidency to boost his personal wealth.

At Sunday's dinner, Trump maintained that his support for cryptocurrencies was to boost American economic competitiveness.

"I'm doing it for the United States ... If we don't do it, China's going to do it," he told attendees, The Guardian reported. The event also featured speeches from boxing champion Floyd Mayweather, "Shark Tank" investor Kevin O'Leary and NBA champion Tristan Thompson.

A total of 185 "wallets" met the criteria for entry to the gala, although the leaderboard on the $TRUMP coin club website listed just 130 wallets. The wallets listed on the leaderboard held $8.6 million in $TRUMP tokens, a sharp decline from the May 2025 gala, which featured 220 attendees holding a combined $148 million. Crypto billionaire Justin Sun's wallet alone held $18.5 million at the 2025 event — more than the combined holdings of this year's attendees.

Eligibility was based on time-weighted holdings of Trump's meme coins from Sept. 30 to Nov. 16, according to the organizer's website. The top 29 holders were invited to a VIP reception with the president, with the top four receiving an 18-karat, Trump-branded gold watch.

More than half of the wallets that qualified for the event were linked to crypto exchanges that bar U.S.-based users, according to data gathered by consumer advocacy group Public Citizen for Reuters, suggesting they belong to foreign nationals.

The $TRUMP token has earned the Trump family at least $635 million, according to the president's June financial disclosure. Including the meme coins, the Trump family has earned $2.3 billion from its four main crypto-related businesses since the president's second term began, through to April of this year, according to a Reuters report.

This is despite the ventures' falling value, which, according to the report, they invest little to no money in. Trump's stake in a fifth crypto-linked venture, Trump Media & Technology Group, is worth about $430 million as of Friday's close.

Bitcoin climbed back to over $61,000 on Monday in line with the U.S. stock futures bounce, after an AI-related stock sell-off spilled over into other riskier assets last week.

Bitcoin fell as low as $55,130 on Saturday, its lowest level since September 2024. The sell-off wiped out $1.8 billion in leveraged crypto positions, largely from bets on prices rising. Leveraged buyers were forced out as prices fell, then returned after the price stabilized.

The world's largest cryptocurrency is still trading at less than half of its October 2025 value. U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs lost $1.24 billion last week, their third week of outflows, according to SoSo Value data cited by FxPro.

#2759. Winners and losers from Ravens’ Week 11 loss to the Panthers

The Baltimore Ravens lost their sixth consecutive game with a 35-33 loss to the Carolina Panthers, and we all lost by watching it.

WR Devontez Walker: The third-year receiver got his first shot as a kick returner this week, and he made the most of it with an 86-yard return for a touchdown. Walker also added a 34-yard catch on offense.

OLB Mike Green: Green followed up his two-sack performance from last week with another pair on Sunday. The former second-round pick appears to be forming into the impact edge rusher many thought he could be.

CB T.J. Tampa: Tampa had the team's lone defensive turnover with an interception in the end zone. This was the first interception by a Ravens cornerback this season. His 90.5 PFF grade was the highest among the team as well. The third-year corner has made a case to remain on the field going forward, even with Marlon Humphrey potentially returning next week.

WR Zay Flowers: Flowers bounced back from his game-losing fumble last week with a solid performance against the Panthers, leading the team with 97 receiving yards on eight catches.

WR/PR LaJohntay Wester: Wester may have lost his job as the Ravens' primary punt returner with two muffs against Carolina. Only one of his fumbles was recovered by the Panthers, but it resulted in a touchdown in a game that was ultimately decided by two points.

RB Justice Hill: It is a shame to include Hill on this list, as he had a strong day outside of a costly fumble through the back of the endzone at the end of a 36-yard run. The play resulted in a Panthers touchback instead of a Ravens touchdown that would have given Baltimore a two-score lead.

TE Mark Andrews: Andrews caught just two passes for 14 yards this week after leading the team with eight catches for 82 yards last week. A lack of production in the passing game is not ideal, but what really landed the veteran on this list was yet another false start. Andrews now has six false starts on the season — tied for second most in the NFL.

Kick coverage unit: Baltimore's special teams have been a weakness all season, and the unit had another poor showing against Carolina. Alongside Wester's two fumbles, the Ravens allowed two 36-yard returns and a 37-yard return on kickoffs.

HC Jesse Minter: Another week, another disappointing outing for Minter's defense. The rookie head coach has been dealt a bad hand with Lamar Jackson missing most of the season, but the side of the ball he was supposed to elevate has failed to improve from a year ago. Minter's game management has been poor during several games this season as well, including on Sunday against the Panthers, with a questionable lack of timeout usage in a two-minute drill at the end of the game. Minter will almost assuredly be back for a second season in Baltimore, but it is safe to say his leash will be tighter than anyone could have expected after just one year.

#2756. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2754. Average 401(k), IRA balances fall as more workers tap their accounts, Fidelity says

Retirement account balances fell slightly in the third quarter amid market volatility, according to data released Monday from Fidelity Investments, the nation's largest provider of 401(k) savings plans.

Some workers also tapped their retirement accounts for cash, signaling that persistent affordability pressures continue to strain household finances, said Mike Shamrell, Fidelity's vice president of thought leadership.

The average 401(k) balance slipped 2% from the previous quarter to $152,500, while the average individual retirement account balance was down 1% to $142,261, Fidelity found. Both figures hit record highs during the second quarter.

Despite the third-quarter decline, average balances in IRAs, 401(k)s and 403(b) plans — which are 401(k)-type plans offered by entities such as public schools, churches and nonprofits — are higher year over year and continue to trend up over the long run.

Wall Street's major averages finished the third quarter on mixed footing, driven in part by the war with Iran, which blocked passage through the Strait of Hormuz, a key transit artery for global energy supplies, and caused oil prices to spike. The stock market's slowdown in the third quarter marked a cooling after the double-digit gains posted in the previous quarter.

The S&P 500 index added 2.03% over the three-month period, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average fell 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite ended the quarter 2.2% higher.

At the same time, financial pressures pushed more savers to reduce their contributions to retirement plans.

The average 401(k) contribution rate, including employee and employer contributions, edged down to 14.3% from 14.4% in the second quarter, Fidelity found. Fidelity's recommended combined annual savings benchmark is 15%.

"We know that there is a lot of concern out there ... [and] people are likely making changes to their personal financial world," Shamrell said.

Some savers also dipped into their accounts to free up cash, a sign of more acute financial strain amid persistently high prices and ongoing inflationary pressures.

The share of workers with an outstanding loan in the third quarter was 19.6%, up slightly from 19.5% in the previous quarter, according to Fidelity. The percentage of participants taking a new loan from their 401(k) held steady at 2.8%.

Separately, the share of workers taking a hardship withdrawal rose to 2.17% from 2.12% in the second quarter, Fidelity found.

"There is a rise because of economic reasons," said Philip Chao, a certified financial planner and chief investment officer at Experiential Wealth, based in Cabin John, Maryland. "No question about it."

A hardship withdrawal can be taken from a retirement plan without paying an early withdrawal penalty as long as the investor has an "immediate and heavy financial need," according to the IRS, such as avoiding foreclosure, eviction or unforeseen health expenses. Such distributions are also typically taxable.

So-called leakage from 401(k) plans, particularly when workers cash out their accounts before retirement, can significantly erode long-term wealth by undermining the power of compound growth. Withdrawing funds during a market downturn can be especially damaging, making it more difficult to recover losses and benefit from a future rebound, financial advisors say.

However, despite the general consensus that savers shouldn't tap their retirement accounts, there may be situations in which investors don't have another good option, Chao said.

In other words, the financial "ideal" doesn't align with "reality" for households, he said.

Retirement plan withdrawals are happening as rising inflation has pressured households' wages and savings, other data also shows.

"Real" average hourly earnings — or wages after accounting for the rising cost of living — fell 0.7% in the year through September 2026, according to the latest annual figures published by the Bureau of Labor Statistics.

Households are saving at their lowest rate since 2022, and consumers are increasingly turning to debt such as credit cards to make ends meet.

Fidelity said earlier this year that 401(k) investors took more loans in 2025 relative to 2024. The findings are consistent with other reports as well.

Vanguard Group, another 401(k) administrator, said in an annual report that savers took a record number of hardship withdrawals last year.

A separate report issued this month by the Plan Sponsor Council of America, a trade group representing employers, found that the share of 401(k) participants with a loan outstanding from their workplace retirement plan rose "significantly" in 2025, nearly doubling from about 12% the prior year.

The percentage of 401(k) investors who took a hardship withdrawal from their plan also increased for the third consecutive year, to 3.4% in 2025 from 1.5% in 2022, according to the PSCA report.

Such "401(k) loans and hardships are there for participants when they need to tap the funds," said Hattie Greenan, the PSCA's research director. "Clearly there's a need right now."

There are situations in which a retirement plan withdrawal could be OK for workers, Chao said.

For example, 401(k) loans are generally a cheaper form of borrowing than credit cards, because investors ultimately pay themselves back with interest, he said.

"If you just need it for a month, two, three, that's fine," Chao said. "It's not going to kill you, borrowing from yourself."

However, a 401(k) loan may make sense only if the saver believes they'll be able to repay it, he said.

Failure to repay a loan can lead to income taxes and penalties that erode long-term savings, Chao said.

Depending on the specific terms of a workplace retirement plan, investors who are laid off or leave their job may have to repay their full loan relatively quickly. Just 34% of plans allow for continued repayment of a loan after separation from service, according to the PSCA report.

#2752. 3 quick takeaways from 49ers win over Vikings: Brock Purdy’s processing was off the charts

The San Francisco 49ers beat up on the Minnesota Vikings 35-10 on Sunday Night Football in Mexico City, improving their record to 9-1 and creating a gap in the NFC.

With the win, the 49ers remained in sole possession of first place in the NFC, while the Vikings dropped to sixth place. Now, it's the Detroit Lions and Chicago Bears who are tied for second place with an 8-2 record.

San Francisco dominated from the jump in this one, and they held a three-score lead by halftime. They forced two turnovers, mitigated the run game, and got pressure on the quarterback on defense, while the offense flowed through the passing game.

Here are three quick takeaways from the 49ers win over the Vikings in Week 11.

Brock Purdy has had some phenomenal performances this season. He started off the season strong against the Los Angeles Rams after an early interception in that game.

The next week, he completed 20 of 22 passes for 287 yards and three total touchdowns against the Miami Dolphins in a 22-point win. He had four passing touchdowns against the Arizona Cardinals. And just last week, he threw for 389 yards and three scores in a 46-40 win over the Cowboys.

This one might've been his most impressive performance of them all. Purdy was just pristine with his timing in the passing game on Sunday. He was seeing things extremely well against the best defense in the league and letting it rip, with the ball placement being elite as well.

Credit goes to Kyle Shanahan as well for a phenomenal game plan and well-called game as well. But Purdy was poised, in rhythm, and on a heater on Sunday night.

When kept clean, he got the ball out quickly (2.48 second time-to-throw) and completed 15 of 18 passes for 170 yards and three touchdowns. On the nine dropbacks he was pressured on, though, Purdy still found a way to complete 6 of 8 passes for 85 yards.

Brian Flores looked to dial up the blitz at an extremely high rate, as always. But he did so even more on Sunday night, as Purdy was blitzed on nearly 89 percent of his dropbacks in the win. The result? He threw for 231 yards and two touchdowns while completing 19 of 23 passes against the blitz, completely dicing up Minnesota's defense.

We've seen Purdy make magic this season time and time again by extending plays with his feet. He did have a few of those plays again in this game, but the way he played with timing and saw the field against a challenging, blitz-heavy defense was huge for the 49ers on Sunday.

This was one of the best quarterback performances of the year, given the opponent. And Purdy did all that while taking only one sack, something much different than last week.

Coming into the game, it was going to be hard to get pressure on and sack Kirk Cousins. This season, Cousins has been very adept at getting the ball out quickly and avoiding sacks, taking only 14 total before Sunday night.

But the 49ers pass rush, which had admittedly been going through its ups and downs, delivered as the game went on Sunday night, coming up with 21 total pressures.

Cousins held the ball more than he usually did and the 49ers pressured him on 16 of his 40 dropbacks (40 percent). And while there was only one sack, Cousins averaged just 4.9 yards per attempt when pressured, while completing only 53 percent of his passes.

The Vikings quarterback was actually also quite good against the blitz, getting the ball out quickly and completing 10 of 13 passes for 132 yards. But when the 49ers brought only four, Cousins struggled, completing only 64 percent of his passes and averaging a measly 4.1 yards per attempt while having a passer rating of 55.9.

Nick Bosa had a strong game just days after coming out and saying he needed to be better. He had five pressures with a 23.3 percent pass-rush win rate, according to PFF, and he wasn't alone. Osa Odighizuwa had four pressures, half a sack, and a 21.7 percent pass-rush win rate. Romello Height had four pressures and a 21.7 percent pass rush win rate. CJ West and Keion White both had two pressures and good win rates.

It was just a better day for the defensive line, who won more with four rushing and still stopped the run, with Minnesota totaling just 69 yards and 3.3 yards per carry on the ground.

The 49ers suffered arguably their most brutal injury of the season so far, as right tackle Colton McKivitz is now out for the year with a fractured ankle, with Kyle Shanahan comparing it to Fred Warner's last year.

McKivitz, a fifth-round pick in the 2020 NFL Draft, had developed from a replacement-level play to one of the best tackles in all of football. Per SumerSports, McKivitz had the third-best pass block win rate of any tackle this season and the third-best positive run block rate. That's going to be incredibly difficult to replace.

San Francisco signed Vederian Lowe to be the team's swing tackle for this type of situation. He had a good game in relief of McKivitz, allowing zero pressures on 18 pass-blocking snaps. He's got experience, having started 25 games so far in his career with the New England Patriots.

But he's got tall shoes to fill, and the injury puts a damper on the biggest win San Francisco has had yet.

Da Silva is my top Tuesday-and-Saturday pickup. Washington only plays twice, but both games are on the light nights: Tuesday against Boston and Saturday against Orlando. He's a stocks play, averaging 1.1 steals and 1.1 blocks in 24.9 minutes this season, with 5.5 rebounds and 1.3 blocks over his last six games. Kelly Oubre Jr., SF/PF, Indiana Pacers (36% rostered): A top-55 player over the last two weeks in 9-cat, Oubre is producing 15.7 points, 1.2 steals and 1.0 blocks, with at least 14 points in six straight games. He's worth holding as long as he keeps starting. Indy has a weird schedule, playing on Monday and then a back-to-back on Friday and Saturday.

Cameron Johnson, SF/PF, Denver Nuggets (33% rostered): He's averaging 13.2 points, 1.3 steals and 1.5 3s in 28.8 minutes over his last six games. Johnson plays Tuesday.

Kevin Huerter, SG/SF, Detroit Pistons (25% rostered): He's played 36-plus minutes in four straight with Duncan Robinson (quad) out, and he's been a top-65 player over the last two weeks (15.3 points, 3.7 assists, 1.4 blocks). You can start him on Tuesday and drop him afterward.

Isaac Okoro, SG/SF, Chicago Bulls (10% rostered): Okoro is playing way better as a starter, averaging 10.1 points, 3.9 rebounds, 2.5 assists, 1.1 steals, and 0.3 blocks in 31.0 minutes over his last eight games. There's no need to rush to get him since you'd only play him on Saturday versus Indy.

Kenrich Williams, PF/C, Oklahoma City Thunder (8% rostered): With so many injuries in OKC's frontcourt, Williams ranks 98th over the past two weeks in 9-cat. He gets steals, doesn't turn over the ball and chips in low-end stats across categories, but nothing to hurt you. Again, you can wait since the only opportunity to stream will be when OKC plays on Saturday.

Brooklyn, Milwaukee and Philadelphia are the only four-game teams with a game on a light night (Saturday). The other six (Clippers, Memphis, Minnesota, Phoenix, Portland and San Antonio) only play on the crowded Monday, Wednesday, Friday and Sunday slates, so in daily leagues you'll have to bench someone for one of these guys. And it could be warranted.

Spencer was in my Week 5 rotation and he's still the best multi-week guard I'd grab if he's out there. He had 26 points on 9-of-11 shooting with 5 3s and 7 assists at Cleveland on Wednesday, then dished out 11 assists against Golden State on Thursday. Over the last two weeks, he's at 13.6 points, 6.5 assists and 2.1 3s on 58% shooting, good for top-45 value in 9-cat. Ty Jerome is back so that will lessen his impact. But he remains one of the top backups to hold in fantasy. It hasn't gone unnoticed that the Grizzlies offense is better with Spencer running point over Jerome.

The backcourt is a mess, but Burries is another four-game add who can be a multi-week hold. The Bucks are 1-16 through Sunday, so the more losses come, the more we will see the young prospects play. Burries is coming off his best game, scoring 26 points with 8 rebounds, 4 assists and 2 3s in 26 minutes Saturday at Philadelphia. He's been a top-125 player over the last two weeks, averaging 12.5 points, 5.8 rebounds, 2.9 assists, and 1.0 steal per game. Milwaukee is one of only three four-game teams that play Saturday, so you'll get something out of him this week, too.

Julian Champagnie, SF/PF, San Antonio Spurs (34% rostered): Champagnie has played 27.6 minutes per game over the last two weeks, averaging 11.0 points and 2.7 3s, with 0.6 steals and 0.6 blocks. If you need 3s and stocks, he can help as the week progresses.

Jaylen Wells, SG/SF/PF, Memphis Grizzlies (21% rostered): Beyond the three-position eligibility, Wells is at 11.9 points and 2.4 3s over his last eight contests. I prefer him for 9-cat formats to take advantage of his efficiency, shooting 48% from the field and 93% from the line over the past two weeks.

Anfernee Simons, PG/SG, Philadelphia 76ers (11% rostered): A points-and-3s play who gets you a Saturday game on a four-game week. He scored 18 in each of his last two games and is at 2.6 3s on 52% shooting over his last seven.

Egor Dëmin, PG/SG, Brooklyn Nets (20% rostered): I'm eyeing Saturday's matchup against Charlotte. He's at 14.0 points and 2.4 3s over his last five, but on 39% shooting, and his role expands if Nolan Traoré (hamstring) sits.

Jordan Goodwin, PG/SG, Phoenix Suns (6% rostered): A top-100 player over the last two weeks with 5.8 rebounds, 3.0 assists and 1.2 steals in 22.3 minutes.

#2748. Patriots reveal plan at QB after Drake Maye injury

The Patriots are sticking with Tommy Cutlets.

Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel indicated the team is not looking to add another quarterback when asked about the possibility of recently released Daniel Jones filling in the shoes of Drake Maye, who went down with an ankle injury during their Week 10 loss to the Lions in Munich.

"Tommy's our starter. Tommy's our starter," Vrabel told the media after the team's bye week on Monday. "We're excited about that. [He had a] good preseason. And Tommy's improved. I know that going into the last game, I think that being thrown in there, he had some good plays, had some things that he can improve on and little things. I think that was probably the biggest thing, just saying, 'Hey, getting back into game action, how do you work in the pocket?' These are things that he got so much better on when he got here.

"So, starting with those things and managing the offense and being able to get us in and out of the huddle and the right plays and just get into a flow and a tempo. And I think that some of the things that we saw in training camp, a lot of those guys that he'll be out there throwing to some of those guys that he worked with in training camp. So, I think that there's some carryover there."

New England Patriots quarterback Tommy DeVito (16) throws against Detroit Lions linebacker Derrick Barnes (55) on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026, in Munich, Germany. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

DeVito's fellow former Giants quarterback, Jones, was released by the Colts on Friday after what has turned into a lost-cause season at 3-8. He was expected to draw interest from the Cardinals, Ravens and Patriots.

He seemingly cleared waivers and became a free agent on Monday, but is reportedly unlikely to sign immediately and will rather wait for the right opportunity.

In the wake of confusion surrounding Maye's injury, the 5-5 Patriots are turning to their backup, who endured a rough relief outing against the Lions.

DeVito completed 12 of 24 passes for 81 yards with one touchdown and one interception in the 35-10 loss.

"Not good enough are the words I would say," DeVito said of his efforts after the game. "Offensively we just never really got going and not really were able to get into a rhythm or get drives going. Too many three-and-outs, just kind of hurting ourselves. … We need to do better at sustaining drives."

New England Patriots quarterback Drake Maye (10) walks on the sideline after an injury in Munich, Germany, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. AP Photo/Doug Benc

New England also has rookie Behren Morton, a Texas Tech product and seventh-round pick.

"I think that this is also a great opportunity for Behren to improve in those reps that him and Tommy may have been splitting or Tommy taking the majority of those," Vrabel added. "I think that that's a great opportunity for Behren to improve in that regard of running the show team and doing those things.

"So, we will look at everything. But again, I don't think that that's something that's going to happen here in the next few days."

Maye exited the international clash early after taking a sack from Aidan Hutchinson, who suffered a worrisome knee injury celebrating the play.

The 24-year-old does not have a definitive timeline for his return nor has the team given information regarding the nature of the injury.

The Patriots travel to face the Chargers (1-9) on Sunday.
