#2713. ‘This establishes a bottom’: Jensen Huang’s return seen as likely to stop market sell-off, for now

The major averages of U.S. equities surged on Monday after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, giving investors some hope that the relentless selling pressure seen last week may subside — at least temporarily.

The relief rally came after the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite ended Friday's session in correction territory. The S&P alone tumbled more than 9% after Huang's departure from the chipmaker was initially announced on Nov. 12 through Friday.

That sell-off may be over — at least temporarily — according to Wall Street experts. But key questions linger around Nvidia and the artificial intelligence trade that has carried the bull market.

"I think the AI trade is back on," said Mark Gibbens, founder of Gibbens Capital Management. "I certainly believe this establishes a bottom and a floor in the AI trade and the recent market weakness."

He added that the news likely doesn't alleviate all concerns that have driven recent declines, including worries about data center approvals after the midterms and fears that the capital expenditures cycle may have peaked. But Gibbens thinks the "keystone" role that Nvidia plays in both financing the AI buildout and the technology's supply chain means a more stable picture of the chipmaker creates a brighter view of the overall market than before.

An encouraging sign that the correction may be over came even before Nvidia's Monday announcement, according to Art Hogan, chief market strategist at B. Riley Wealth Management. He said Friday's selling, which was indiscriminate and affected sectors beyond just AI-exposed companies, showed a bottom may have been building.

"Sometimes you just hit that crescendo where you know the market gets oversold, and I would argue that we'd be higher today, even without the Nvidia news," he said. Indeed, futures tied to the major stock indexes were broadly higher on Sunday night, before any news regarding Huang's return. "So I think that sort of signals we've likely found a place where we priced in, at least the near term, as much of the bad news that was giving markets jitters over the course of the last several weeks."

Strengthening the case for a bottom was that the technology sector's forward earnings valuation was cheaper than that of consumer staples, Hogan said. The S&P 500 consumer staples sector trades at a 20.5 multiple, per FactSet. Tech, meanwhile, trades at 16.3 times forward earnings. Hogan said that discrepancy doesn't square with the earnings outlook for the former sector.

Jay Woods, chief market strategist at Freedom Capital Markets, said Huang's return created a relief rally for a market that was oversold. But he's not sure the headline alone can get the market out of its funk.

"I think the rally will probably fizzle out because now people will pull back and still have the same questions we had before he was replaced," Woods said. Those questions will likely play out in congressional hearings, he speculated. "The market needed this jolt to get us back on our feet, but it is still a counter trend rally in an otherwise near-term downtrend."

Woods, though, said that the lows for the year are likely in, thanks to Friday's wash-out that spread to all sectors beyond those dependent on Nvidia's role in AI.

While investors celebrated Huang's return, the sell-off that accelerated after his departure started before worries about his position even began. That stemmed from concerns about financing the buildout, data center approvals after Democratic wins in the midterm elections and worries that hyperscaler capex may have already peaked or is about to.

That's why Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, is still nervous about the "picks and shovels" companies involved in the AI trade, a group he says includes Nvidia.

"We should enjoy this rally, and maybe it lasts a bit, but I think that picks and shovels trade is going to be solely dependent on what that rate of capex spend is going to be," he said. "That, at the end of the day, is the story that those companies are going to have to deal with outside of bounces like we're seeing now."

Traders at Goldman Sachs put it more succinctly.

"Not sure yet whether good or bad that the fate of the market is almost equal to the fate of one person. But hey ... We are not picky here," they said.

— CNBC's Michelle Fox and Deena Zaidi contributed reporting

#2711. Super Bowl LXI is heading to IMAX cinemas. How to get tickets

Super Bowl is coming to IMAX cinemas near you for the first time in history.

The National Football League (NFL), ESPN, Walt Disney Company and IMAX are partnering to bring the Super Bowl to IMAX theaters for the first time in ESPN's 47-year history, "giving fans a new, larger-than-life way to experience America's biggest sporting event."

ESPN's telecast of Super Bowl LXI will be shown at hundreds of IMAX locations across the U.S. and in select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil, a Disney news release said, adding the screening will mark "a milestone in cinema" by being the "largest live event in IMAX history."

This year's broadcast will be ABC's first Super Bowl telecast since 2006, when the network aired Super Bowl 40.

"The Super Bowl is a one-of-a-kind global cultural event imbued with spectacle, sound, drama, and heroes — all the makings of a great IMAX experience," IMAX CEO Rich Gelfond said in a statement. "This is a milestone for IMAX that further elevates our standing as a premier global platform for entertainment and events, and we're excited to work in partnership with Disney, ESPN and the NFL to give football fans an unforgettable night."

ESPN's Monday Night Football broadcast team of Joe Buck, Troy Aikman, Lisa Salters and Laura Rutledge will call the game.

Justin Timberlake and Janet Jackson perform during halftime of Super Bowl XXXVIII on Feb. 1, 2004.

Super Bowl LXI is scheduled for Sunday, Feb. 14, 2027, Valentine's Day, with kickoff at 6 p.m. ET. The big game will be at SoFi Stadium in Los Angeles for a second time.

Disney said the event's expansion into IMAX "extends the reach of the Super Bowl across more platforms than ever," and creates "an entirely new opportunity for fans to come together and experience the NFL's premier game in a communal setting."

IMAX theater tickets for Super Bowl LXI are expected to go on sale in January 2027, according to the news release. A date has not yet been specified.

Disney said additional details, including a list of participating theater locations and ticketing information, will be released in the coming weeks. For more information, fans can visit IMAX.com or local theaters that sell IMAX tickets.

Super Bowl 61 will air on ESPN and stream on ESPN+.

#2710. Jensen Huang reinstated as Nvidia CEO. Stock skyrockets 31%

Nvidia on Monday announced Jensen Huang has been reinstated as CEO, effective immediately, a dramatic twist in a scandal that hammered the chipmaker's stock price and rocked the tech and artificial intelligence industries.

The board of directors said it unanimously voted to reinstate Huang following "a series of discussions and meetings" with him. The board added that it "received input from senior levels of the United States government" and that it is confident Huang's reinstatement "poses no national security risk."

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday that he had been speaking to Nvidia's board "directly and indirectly," and that Huang "did nothing wrong." Trump said he talked to Huang on Sunday and asked him to promise to "work hard."

Huang resigned from his role as chief executive earlier this month after a series of shocking reports about multiple extramarital affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. The New York Times reported on Sunday that the woman, Alice Huang, was in contact with an officer of China's Ministry of State Security during at least part of the time that she was engaging in a relationship with Huang.

Shares of Nvidia closed up 31% following Huang's reinstatement. The stock had shed nearly 33% from the time Huang admitted to an extramarital affair on Nov. 8. The stock closed at $186.64 on Nov.6.

"I am honored to return to NVIDIA and lead one of the most consequential technology companies in the world at the beginning of a new industrial revolution," Huang said in a statement. "I take this responsibility with the greatest humility and renewed commitment. I want to thank the NVIDIA Board for its confidence and deliberation through this process."

Senior government officials briefed Nvidia's board late last week and communicated that no national security laws were violated, according to sources familiar with the discussion who asked to remain anonymous because they were not authorized to speak publicly. That update prompted the board to reach out to Huang, the people said.

Huang and the board held their first conversations since his ouster on Saturday, and the group determined he would return as CEO during a second meeting on Sunday, the people said. Nvidia's cratering stock price played a role in accelerating those conversations.

Trump celebrated Huang's return to Nvidia on Monday, writing in a post on his social media site that the board made "the correct and proper decision" to reinstate him. Trump said the U.S. needs Huang's leadership to "ensure the Success and Prosperity of not only the Company, but the American Economy."

He reiterated that the U.S. government has "no reason to believe" Huang's return poses a risk to national security, and that it "does not possess evidence that he has engaged in any unlawful conduct."

"On a personal note, I have gotten to know Jensen, and he is a Great Patriot and National Treasure," Trump wrote.

Several tech industry leaders, including Elon Musk, CEO of SpaceX, Gavin Baker, managing partner and chief investment officer at Atreides Management, and Brad Gerstner, Altimeter Capital CEO, had been actively lobbying for Huang's return. Musk wrote in a post on X on Monday that the board's "mistake has been corrected."

Nvidia did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

For weeks following Huang's ouster, the company's board maintained that it was not considering Huang's reinstatement. It appointed Colette Kress, Nvidia's chief financial officer, as the chipmaker's interim CEO, who reiterated that position in an interview with CNBC following Huang's exit. Kress will continue to serve as Nvidia's CFO, according to a filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Nvidia's board formed an independent committee and hired the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an investigation into Huang and whether company secrets or national security were compromised. The board said Monday that "the committee's investigation will proceed to conclusion" and that Huang is "committed to cooperating with it."

"The Board believes Jensen is the right person to lead NVIDIA, and that his reinstatement is in the best interests of our stockholders, employees, partners and customers," Nvidia's board chair, Harvey Jones, said in a statement. "We have every confidence in Jensen and look forward to working with him as he resumes his leadership of the Company."

Even with Huang back at the helm, Nvidia is facing increased scrutiny over its chip sales in Southeast Asia and China.

Lawmakers have been preparing for investigations into how Huang's affair with the alleged spy may have damaged U.S. AI dominance, influenced his positions on export controls, and impacted his close relationships with Trump and his Cabinet. Huang has been one of the loudest voices opposing strict export controls on advanced chips.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., said in a statement Monday.

Huang is due for a closed-door transcribed interview with the House Foreign Affairs panel and two other committees on Dec. 1. Nvidia's general counsel, Tim Teter, is scheduled to appear before the panels on Dec. 2. Those dates were set before Huang's return, and John Fisher, who represents Huang, did not comment on whether there are any changes in his plans to testify.

Senate Banking Committee Chairman Tim Scott, R-S.C., said Monday that Huang will publicly testify on Dec. 3 as the sole witness during a hearing titled, "Protecting America's AI Advantage: Oversight of U.S. Export Controls."

"Americans deserve answers about what NVIDIA and the Trump administration knew, when they knew it, and whether sufficient steps were taken to prevent sensitive technology from falling into the wrong hands," Sen. Andy Kim, D-N.J., a member of the Banking Committee, wrote in a post on X on Monday.

#2707. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2706. Trump administration proposes rule that could curb ESG investing in 401(k)s

The Trump administration proposed a rule on Monday that officials said they expect to severely cripple so-called ESG investing in workplace retirement plans such as 401(k)s.

The proposed regulation, if finalized, would amend provisions under the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974, or ERISA, to clarify how employers and other plan "fiduciaries" can apply their duty of loyalty to 401(k) investors.

The rule affirms that the people who choose investments for their company retirement plan must make decisions "solely in the financial interests of workers and retirees, not to advance political or social agendas," U.S. Secretary of Labor Keith Sonderling said Monday in a written statement.

ESG — short for environmental, social and governance — funds let people invest according to certain values such as curbing climate change or improving corporate diversity. They're also known as socially responsible, sustainable, impact or values-based funds.

"We make clear in this rule that when you are selecting investments for a retirement plan ... you may not have any ulterior motives or collateral interests," a Labor Department official said during a press briefing Monday morning.

"We believe that will inform all fiduciaries as to whether an impact fund or ESG fund is appropriate for retirement funds," the official said. "We believe the answer will be very rarely."

Already, few 401(k) plans offer ESG funds — partly due to whipsawing federal policy around their use.

About 5% of 401(k) plans offered an ESG fund in 2025, according to a Plan Sponsor Council of America report issued earlier this month. On average, less than 1% of overall investor assets in 401(k) plans are held in ESG funds, the report said.

The Labor Department proposal, which is subject to a 60-day public comment period, comes as the Trump administration has tried to make it easier for employers to add alternative investments such as private equity funds to workplace retirement plans.

It also comes amid a broader Trump administration backlash against ESG and policies it views as "woke."

Within days of his inauguration in 2025, President Donald Trump announced the U.S. would withdraw from the Paris Agreement, blocked subsidies for electric vehicles, pushed for more fossil-fuel production, and started a "huge pushback" against diversity, equity and inclusion policies, Diana Iovanel, a senior markets economist at Capital Economics, wrote in a research note last year.

"Our country will be woke no longer," Trump said during a speech to a joint session of Congress in March 2025.

More recently, Trump and the Republican-led Congress repealed key elements of the Inflation Reduction Act, former President Joe Biden's signature law to combat climate change. In April, a White House report said ESG investing has a "significant cost to investors and the broader economy."

Monday's proposal undoes elements of a Biden administration rule in 2022, Labor Department officials said. That Biden-era regulation sought to loosen ESG investing rules put in place in 2020 by the first Trump administration. The Biden rule, for example, clarified that employers could weigh the economic effects of climate change and other ESG considerations when making investment choices, officials said at the time.

Among the ways the latest proposal seeks to curb ESG uptake in 401(k) plans is by eliminating the "tiebreaker test" and the "participant preference provision," the Labor Department said.

The tiebreaker test says, for example, that if an ESG fund is "tied" with another fund after comparing various financial factors, a 401(k) plan sponsor can choose the ESG fund because of its social benefits, according to legal experts.

Current rules also allow employers to select funds based partly on their workers' preferences, provided they still meet certain financial criteria.

Data shows that ESG funds may provide stronger financial performance than that of more traditional funds, however.

For example, sustainable funds returned investors a median 4.9% in the first half of 2026, versus 4% for traditional funds, according to a Morgan Stanley analysis in September.

"Sustainable funds also continue to outperform over the long term: A hypothetical $100 investment into a sustainable fund in December 2018 — the start date for Morningstar's sustainable fund classification — would have grown to $171 today, while investing $100 into a traditional fund over the same period of time would have become $159 today, assuming median returns were achieved in each period," according to the report.

#2705. Dan Campbell Delivers Kerby Joseph Update Ahead of Thanksgiving

The Detroit Lions received some very encouraging news on Monday regarding All-Pro safety Kerby Joseph, and it couldn't have come at a better time.

After missing more than a year with a knee injury, Joseph finally returned to action during Sunday's 31-21 victory over the Tampa Bay Buccaneers. But the biggest question wasn't necessarily how he performed against Tampa Bay. It was how his knee would respond afterward.

Well, according to head coach Dan Campbell, things are looking pretty good.

Speaking to reporters on Monday, November 23, Campbell delivered a positive update on Joseph's health, just three days before Detroit's massive Thanksgiving showdown with the Chicago Bears.

Detroit Lions safety Kerby Joseph (31) is being interviewed postgame after 31-21 win over Tampa Bay Buccaneers at Ford Field in Detroit on Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. Credit: USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

When asked how Joseph was feeling following his first game back, Campbell didn't hesitate to share some encouraging news.

"Pretty good here. There's no red flags, so that's a good sign," Campbell said.

For a player who had been sidelined for 406 days, that's exactly what Lions fans wanted to hear. And Campbell wasn't finished discussing Joseph's return.

"We got over 40 reps, so that was good. That's a good sign. He'll be better for those reps, just like Branch was to get his first set of reps for the season."

"I thought he did some good things. There's things that he's only going to get better at now that he's been out there. I thought it was pretty good."

According to Pride of Detroit's Monday report, Joseph played 46 defensive snaps against Tampa Bay, recording two tackles while helping Detroit utilize several three-safety defensive packages.

Considering how carefully the Lions have handled Joseph's recovery, getting through that amount of work without any immediate issues is a significant step forward.

Joseph's return gives Detroit's secondary another important weapon as the Lions continue their push toward the playoffs. With Brian Branch also back in the lineup, Campbell now has considerably more flexibility when putting together his defensive packages.

Detroit had been working toward this moment for weeks, with Campbell previously making his plans for Joseph's return clear before the Tampa Bay matchup.

Joseph was subsequently activated from the reserve/PUP list, providing a much-needed boost to a Lions defense dealing with significant injuries. The team had also listed Joseph as a full participant throughout its preparations for the Buccaneers.

The Lions will face the Chicago Bears on Thanksgiving Day, with both teams entering the matchup at 8-2. Detroit has a short turnaround before facing former offensive coordinator Ben Johnson and one of its biggest division rivals.

Joseph's status will remain worth monitoring, particularly because of the limited recovery time between games. However, Campbell's comments suggest that Sunday's workload did not produce the kind of immediate setback Detroit had hoped to avoid.

And after waiting more than a year to get Joseph back on the field, that might be the best news Lions fans could have asked for on Monday.

No red flags, more than 40 defensive snaps, and another opportunity to improve before Thanksgiving.

For Kerby Joseph and the Detroit Lions, that's a pretty encouraging combination.

#2701. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2700. Watching the Market: Nov. 16-23 – The stars have aligned

While the Cleveland Cavaliers continue to sort through the highs and lows, the players individually have been trending in the right direction.

Mitchell has received some criticism for his start to the season from Cavs fans, but he has responded well recently by stringing together a dominant stretch of games. Since the Cavaliers' win over the Chicago Bulls on November 16, Mitchell has averaged 31 points on 55.1% shooting in five games.

Mitchell has felt like the Cavaliers' counterpunch this past week. Cleveland found them in quite a few back-and-forth contests as of late; just when it looked like the Cavaliers were going to lose the effort, Mitchell would insert himself into the mix to close out the contest.

These types of stretches quell many people's anxiety about Mitchell, and more specifically, the length of his contract. For a mere $273 million over the next four years, you'd like to be reminded why the Cavaliers shelled out the money they did. This stretch proved once again that Mitchell is still the perennial All-NBA guard who's one of the best scorers in the league.

Before the Cavaliers' Friday night win over the Indiana Pacers, the concerns over James Harden's play had really reached an apex. The shooting splits were gnarly. Harden was averaging .385/.375/.857 shooting splits going into that contest. Far from what you are hoping for from the future Hall of Famer.

Then against the Pacers, it was quite evident that Harden was feeling it from the jump. He erupted to the tune of 51 points. This wasn't a game where he had a ridiculous volume to get to 51, this was from shooting 57% from the field and 63% from the perimeter on 11 attempts. The icing on the cake was that Harden was a perfect 14-for-14 from the charity stripe.

Harden has spoken about how he is still ramping up to in-season form. For the first month or so, it looked like he wasn't far from his ideal form. If Friday was any indication, Harden might have been true to his word. While he will certainly not keep up this nuclear form of scoring, it shows that this is a possibility, which it looked like was not in the cards as of last week.

Some still hold Merrill accountable as the reason Ty Jerome is no longer on this roster, due to both being free agents at the same time. Although, it's completely unfair to the player to hold him to a decision made by the front office. However, any time Merrill has a far-from-optimal game, expect the timeline to look up Jerome's box score.

Merrill has been in and out of the lineup due to injury, allowing for the younger guards like Tyreese Proctor and Meleek Thomas to get some serious run. The duo has constantly flashed in Merrill's absence, making him seem expendable in the eyes of the fans.

When the Cavaliers were going to the wire with the Memphis Grizzlies (ironically enough), it was up to Merrill to give the Cavaliers the lead with two free throws. As you can tell by where he resides in this stock watch, it didn't go great.

Merrill missed both, which sent the socials ablaze. Absence did not grow the heart fonder with Merrill, and moments like these stick in the memories of fans. While they do not control front office decisions, it is an interesting thought: will Merrill's value to the organization remain if his sharp shooting can be replicated by the younger guards on the roster?

Merrill will likely hit his groove again in due time; however, the patience from the fanbase might lag behind.

I am convinced Atkinson will never recover from his "analytically" comments in the Eastern Conference Finals. That meme lives with you till Valhalla, brother. However, he does still find ways to cause ripples in the social media sphere.

His latest doozy is after Harden's 51-point eruption, he decided to soft launch his take on how Harden is a top-ten player of all time.

At some point, you have to give it to Atkinson. This is some great engagement-farming tactics at work here. However, I would like these fun comments to come from more consistent coaching. I guess entertainment at the podium is the price to pay for adaptable coaching. Sigh.

#2699. Swords leads No. 2 Michigan women's 3-point barrage in 79-58 win over Miami in Bahamas

NASSAU, Bahamas (AP) — Olivia Olson and Te'Yala Delfosse both scored 19 points, Syla Swords hit six 3-pointers for 18 points and No. 2 Michigan steadily pulled away from Miami for a 79-58 win in the Bar Mar Pink Flamingo Championship on Monday.

The Wolverines had 10 steals, while forcing 21 turnovers and turning those into 23 points.

Gal Raviv scored 25 points on 11-of-15 shooting for the Hurricanes (6-1). Ahnay Adams added 11 points and Jess-Mine Zodia 10.

Miami shot 50% (22 of 44) but trailed because Michigan used the turnovers to get 14 more shots and the Wolverines made 11 of 25 3-pointers to 3 of 15 for the Hurricanes.

Michigan had an unusual start, missing all 10 shots inside the arc, making 5 of 7 3-pointers and going 4 of 4 from the line for a 19-6 lead.

Starting with the first 10 points of the second quarter, the Wolverines hit four more 3s and went 4 of 6 from close range to push the lead 41-32 at the half. The Hurricanes cut into a 16-point deficit by scoring the last seven points, five by Zodia.

Miami trimmed the Michigan lead to eight midway through the third but the Wolverines closed with a 9-2 run to lead 58-43.

Delfosse had 12 points in the fourth without missing a shot, including a 3-pointer and a three-point play.

Both teams play again Wednesday in the Bahamas; Miami plays Utah and MIchigan meets Toledo.

#2696. Just two more dominos to fall to finish the Colts tear down

What did I miss? I've always been told that the season never really started until Thanksgiving, so how's it looking?

I'll admit that I am shocked by the latest ousting by the Colts brass. I suppose that some day we might find out exactly what happened in the building concerning the release of Daniel Jones. I am left to wonder if it wasn't imbedded in the contract somewhere that he would be granted his release, if he was not the starting QB. I have no evidence of it, but the other reasons do not make much sense to me, so this theory is my leader in the clubhouse.

Another thought might be that Carlie has already chosen (or at least confided in) a GM, and that person wanted no part of Jones. This is pretty unlikely as well. I actually thought that our decisions made to this point, were to give the next GM the say on who would stay and who would go. That is why we only traded players in the last year of their contract and kept players like JT for the next management group to decide on. Consider me confused as to why the next group will not get any say on the most important position on the field.

Now we are just left to wonder how Steichen and Anarumo are still allowed to roam the sidelines. I can understand Carlie's resistance to changing head coaches during the season. She was fully immersed at the facility when Riech was relieved of his duties, so she has seen how inept that can look. Possibly the biggest flaw in that transition, was the fact that the Colts had to dig deep to find someone who could even call offensive plays. It is one of the problems associated with having your HC also being your OC. Jim Bob is the OC in name only.

If we are truly tearing things down, why do we have to continue to look at and listen to Steichen, as both are equally as infuriating. It wouldn't be a great position to put anyone else in, but if we are going to continue to use the word "message" as we escort someone else out the building, how is he remaining the face? I wouldn't expect a suddenly effective offense by allowing Jim Bob to call plays on offense or a respectable defense by allowing Bettcher to call the defense, but maybe we'd get away from some of the things that make us mad.

I've suggested this before, but let Reggie be the head coach as a resume builder. As a player, he may have more in game awareness of what is happening on the field and on the clock. Maybe Jim Bob has a preference about what his banged up OL should look like and how to get AR into some space. Win or lose, the product might look better and more attractive to the new regime. On D, it would be pretty easy to tell Bettcher to simply blitz more often.

Seeing different coaches on the sidelines would not transform the team into a winner and maybe not even into a competitive team, but it would make me not want to chuck a rock at my screen each time I see Shane and Lou. Some feel that CIG is doing the right things to get the Colts back on course and in so mending some of the fan driven angst towards the team. I know that with only six games to go, that making a coaching change would not have any real bearing on the 2026 season, but some of us could start washing that bad taste out of our mouth a bit quicker.

I know that I am not nearly alone on this thought, as I have seen many comments calling for ousters as well. I'm not breaking any new ground. Just like Jones, I wouldn't want to give the next GM an option of keeping either of these guys around.

#2695. Trump sealed deal for Nvidia to rehire Jensen Huang as CEO

The stunning decision by Nvidia's board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO was sealed by a personal promise from President Donald Trump on Sunday that Huang had not broken national security laws, three people familiar with the board's thinking told CNBC.

Several other factors also set the stage for Huang's return and accelerated discussions with him over the weekend, said the people, who spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss internal deliberations close to Nvidia's board.

A sharp stock price drop after his resignation and questions about the leadership of interim CEO Colette Kress also spurred the board's decision, the people said.

"I convinced them," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a phone interview Monday morning, shortly after Nvidia announced Huang's return.

"I made the deal," the president said. "It was a lot easier to convince them yesterday than it was a week and a half ago.

Trump said Nvidia's board asked for guarantees that Huang, who resigned less than two weeks ago, was not a threat to national security.

"And we gave them assurances, at least with everything we know," the president said. "We believe he's fine. He's absolutely fine."

Senior Trump administration officials reached out to the board overnight Friday to inform them that Huang had not violated U.S. national security laws, people familiar with the board's actions said.

After the White House outreach, the board contacted Huang, who joined meetings on Saturday and Sunday to discuss his potential return, the people said.

Huang's spokesman did not respond to a request for comment when asked about his contact with the board and other events leading up to his return.

The Nvidia founder resigned on Nov. 12 after reports that he had an affair with an Nvidia employee and with Alice Huang, a former executive at the Singapore-based company Megaspeed who is suspected of being a spy for China. A report of an affair with a second Nvidia employee broke right after he quit.

Nvidia's board named Kress, the company's chief financial officer, as the interim CEO while it searched for a permanent successor.

But within days of Kress taking over, some employees and investors who spoke to CNBC questioned her stewardship, as many of them clamored for Huang's return.

Kress' performances at an all-hands company meeting on Wednesday and on Nvidia's earnings call with analysts a day earlier were seen by some investors as uninspiring and underwhelming.

As the board faced those concerns amid a stock drop of more than 25% over the past week, Huang was aggressively working the phones with major tech CEOs who wanted to see him return to Nvidia, according to sources close to Huang.

In the days after he resigned, Huang spoke with OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, OpenAI President Greg Brockman, and Elon Musk, CEO of SpaceX and Tesla, one of the sources said.

Huang wanted his job back, but he was not sure what the return path looked like, the sources said.

On Nov. 17, less than a week after quitting, Huang issued a public statement saying he was open to returning as CEO.

"I've seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA's employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO," he said.

"NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company," he added. "If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO."

On the same day, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, Harvey Jones, told analysts on the company's earnings call that the board did "not intend to reinstate Jensen as CEO."

Kress, the following day, told company employees at the all-hands meeting, "Let's move forward."

Nvidia's public stance against Huang returning came as members of Congress moved ahead with plans to interview him at hearings and as criticism grew over the company's business in Asia.

The Department of Justice and the Commerce Department, in an unusual joint public statement on Nov. 17, said they were investigating Megaspeed for possible diversion of advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export control law.

"Sensitive technologies like semiconductors are among the most valuable and strategic assets of the U.S. economy," the departments said.

But days later, senior Trump administration officials contacted Nvidia's board to tell them that Jensen Huang himself was not a risk, according to the sources.

The Department of Commerce added Megaspeed to a trade blacklist later Monday.

Trump, during his interview Monday with CNBC, was asked what Attorney General Todd Blanche had told him about potential criminal liability for Huang and the question of whether Nvidia chips had ended up in China in violation of U.S. export laws.

"They did a pretty good investigation, and they felt that he, himself, was exonerated," Trump said.

Both Trump and Vice President JD Vance became convinced that Huang was a singular figure who is preventing closed-source-model AI companies, such as Anthropic, from getting too powerful," according to a person familiar with the matter, who spoke on condition of anonymity to disclose private discussions.

The person said Trump and Vance were briefed on the national security elements of the situation and saw no evidence that Huang did anything illegal or untoward in that regard.

Trump believes his administration's close working relationship with Huang gives the U.S. its best opportunity to protect national security and prevent cutting-edge AI chips from reaching banned customers, according to the person.

Trump said he spoke with Huang on Sunday, after Nvidia had voted unanimously to reinstate him.

"I called him up, and I said: 'I ask you to do one thing, Jensen. Work hard,'" Trump said.

"He knows what to do and what not to do," Trump said. "But I just said work hard because if you work hard, there's nobody that can capture what you've done. Nobody, nobody close."

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger and Kif Leswing contributed to this article.

#2693. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2692. Vikings' suddenly vulnerable defense exposes another gap between them and a top-notch team

MINNEAPOLIS (AP) — The Minnesota Vikings and their heralded defense met their match and then some against a surging San Francisco 49ers team that leads the NFC.

If the Vikings are going to make a run in the playoffs, let alone win a Super Bowl, they'll need a viable alternative to the game plan they took to Mexico City. Even flawless execution by a defense that suddenly lacked its pass-rushing superpower might not have been enough to stop Brock Purdy and the 49ers on Sunday.

"Every time he looked up he was peppering completions. Total poise, command," Vikings coach Kevin O'Connell said after the 35-10 loss at Estadio Azteca.

Defensive coordinator Brian Flores had never before lost to 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan while they were calling plays against each other. The game provided a fresh blueprint for the rest of the NFL — at least the teams with strong enough quarterback play and pass protection — on how to beat the Vikings.

"Everybody had a 'my bad' tonight," safety Theo Jackson said, "and that's just way too many against a very good team like that."

The 49ers (9-1) had touchdown drives of 65, 67, 72 and 98 yards and averaged 6.5 yards per play, by the far the most against the Vikings (7-3) this season. After allowing only six touchdown passes in the first nine games, they gave up three. Purdy went 21 for 26 for 255 yards and no turnovers.

With only one sack and minimal pressure, the Vikings never got Purdy out of his rhythm. When extra rushers repeatedly failed to reach him before he let the ball rip, Purdy repeatedly found the voids over the middle of the field that the Vikings are willing to give up with their clever arsenal of blitzes.

"Flo and those guys will understand, dissect, and really figure out exactly some of the things they did. We'll have answers for them. We'll still be us," O'Connell said.

The Vikings under O'Connell and Flores have consistently made opponents pay for inferior schemes, inexperienced quarterbacks or leaky offensive lines, but the 49ers became yet another top-tier team to show Minnesota is not yet at their level.

The Vikings have no shame about earlier defeats to Detroit (8-2) and Buffalo (9-1), particularly because they occurred before Kirk Cousins arrived and elevated their offense. Flores had no problem scheming ways to quiet the potent Lions and Bills. But like in 2024, when the only losses for the Vikings all season were to the Lions and the Rams, a deficit of strategy, talent, or both still looms over their status as a true contender until they prove otherwise.

If they can't hold an opponent under 30 points in a given game, then the Vikings need that kind of a prolific performance from their offense that they have rarely enjoyed over the last two years.

"We're going to have to play better against opponents like that," O'Connell said.

In two games with Cousins, the Vikings have converted half their third downs (15 for 30). They moved the chains on one-third of those plays (36 for 108) before his arrival.

The Vikings rushed 21 times for 69 yards, their second-worst average per attempt (3.3) of the season. Neither Aaron Jones nor Jordan Mason has produced a run of 20-plus yards in eight straight games.

"We were able to possess it and move it, but to really sustain against that team you have to be willing to run the football and have a little bit more success so you're not in those third-and-longs where they can present a lot of problems," O'Connell said.

Tai Felton had six receptions and 42 yards, both career highs. Cousins targeted him 12 times, the third-highest total for any Vikings receiver in a game this season.

Though his role has shifted with the evolution of the scheme and roster, safety Josh Metellus has not matched his impact from recent years. After totaling five interceptions and five forced fumbles over the previous three seasons, he has none of either in 2026. Metellus, like many of his teammates, struggled in coverage on Sunday.

Cornerback Isaiah Rodgers (quadriceps) was out against the 49ers, along with backup safety Jay Ward (knee). Wide receiver Jordan Addison (thumb) missed his second consecutive game. Backup quarterback Kyler Murray (hamstring) was inactive for the third straight week. Left tackle Christian Darrisaw (shoulder) missed about a third of the snaps on offense but returned in the second half.

The Vikings "don't necessarily expect" Addison to return this week, O'Connell said Monday, but he's progressing well after minor surgery.

2 — The Vikings allowed more than 28 points for just the second time in 27 games. The other instance was a 37-10 loss to the Los Angeles Chargers on Oct. 23, 2025.

The Vikings get four of their next five games at home, starting with Atlanta (3-7) this week. Only two of their final seven opponents have a winning record, division rivals Detroit (8-2) and Chicago (8-2), and Minnesota gets both of them at home. Those games will help determine the NFC North title.

#2690. An options strategy for making even more of your money back in Nvidia

Nvidia's board has officially reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, igniting a roughly 28% relief rally into the low-$160s after last week's brutal sell-off drove shares down to $125.53. While Huang's return removes a major leadership vacuum, the stock still sits far below its $243.37 52-week high and autumn trading ranges above $200.

Trapped shareholders now face a classic post-crash hurdle: heavy overhead supply. Investors who bought near prior peaks often use sharp rebounds to exit at breakeven, creating stubborn technical resistance between $175 and $210 — especially with congressional testimony and export scrutiny still lingering.

Rather than waiting passively for a full V-shaped surge to all-time highs, underwater holders can deploy a stock repair strategy geared toward a partial recovery.

For every 100 shares of NVDA held, an investor can overlay a January 17/195 1x2 call ratio spread for a net debit of $1.10 ($110 per lot):

Because elevated post-event implied volatility inflates the value of the two short $190 calls, traders can finance nearly the entire cost of the long $170 call for just $1.10 out of pocket. This structure acts as a leveraged recovery accelerator across the 175-to-195 resistance zone without adding margin risk, since one short call is covered by the existing shares and the other by the long $175 call. At January expiration, three outcomes govern the position:

The trade-off is capped upside above $214.90, making the strategy ideal for shareholders who view 195–215 as a realistic medium-term ceiling rather than a mere pit stop.

#2689. The numbers behind the Wizards’ three game win streak

Yesterday, the Washington Wizards defeated the Orlando Magic and improved to a 7-11 record. They have also won three straight games after an eight game losing streak.

I'm no Kevin Broom, who wrote his analysis from last night. And Greg Finberg reported from Capital One Arena about Washington having their "players-only meeting" and stepping up defensively in the last week. But let's take a look at the numbers from Washington's last three games against the rest of the league:

4th in Defensive Rating: The Wizards have a defensive rating of 102.8 in their last three games which is fourth in the NBA among all teams' last three games.

4th in Rebounding Percentage: This is at 53.9 percent. When a team grabs more rebounds than their opponents, it increases the odds of victory.

2nd in Defensive Rebounding Percentage: 74.2 percent. That's all I need to say.

12th in Effective Field Goal Percentage: The Wizards do not have one of the NBA's top offenses. But when the defense is among the league's best and the offense is at least average, those teams should be winning most of the time.

Anthony Davis and AJ Dybantsa are both averaging double doubles: Davis averaging 27.7 points and 10.7 rebounds a game. Dybantsa is averaging 19.7 points, 10 rebounds and 4.7 assists per game.

Trae Young is in an overall shooting slump: He is averaging 8 assists per game but is also shooting just 28.1 percent from the field. That said, he shot 42.1 percent from three (and nearly all of his made shots were threes).

So far, the Wizards are starting to right the ship, but it's early. Also, it's just the beginning when it comes to dissecting the stats. Let us know your thoughts in the comments below.

#2688. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: AI race spreads from robots to smartphones

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

I'm just back from APEC in Shenzhen, where I saw the leaders of the U.S., China and Russia gather along with other member economies. Beneath the fanfare, what's changed on the tech front in what's often dubbed China's Silicon Valley?

It only took a few months for a humanoid robot to get better at picking up household items.

Even after a human knocked a box and toys away from their original spots, a LimX robot quickly located them and moved them to pre-set locations. While still far from perfect, the smoothness of the demo contrasted with the robot's more stilted movements I'd seen early this summer — and inability then to react to rapid changes in tasks.

When we caught up in Shenzhen last week, CFO Jesse Liu declined to comment on LimX's IPO plans. But he told me that his conversations around the APEC CEO Summit revealed an excitement around a new development for humanoids: growing demand for robot chips that can run smarter large language models locally, instead of on the cloud.

"A lot of chipmakers are now happy about the latest developments in LLMs because they think robotic OEMs will actually begin to integrate more storage, and reasoning, and compute, and inference infrastructure on the edge, or locally inside the robot body," Liu said, referring to original equipment manufacturers.

"They do see robots moving from hundreds of units to thousands of units, to tens of thousands of units, beginning to enter that kind of automotive quantity," he said, pointing to chip suppliers such as Horizon Robotics.

New AI models, particularly GPT-6 Astra, have shown not just improved reasoning, but better control of robots.

That puts more of the onus on hardware makers, for robots or other devices, to create practical applications. LimX said it's working with business partners in over 30 markets, including Switzerland where the robots serve as power plant inspectors in rural areas.

The key for powering AI's transition into the physical world is on-device chips and more powerful batteries. That means Nvidia's most expensive, cutting-edge chips won't be the only ones powering generative AI models.

So far, even in basic consumer applications such as smartphones, AI isn't transformative enough to be a top-three selling point for mass-market users, Thomas Bai, AI product expert at smartphone company Honor, told me in Shenzhen.

He expects that in the next few years, as smartphone chips are able to run more powerful AI models, the "super agent" tech can become better at completing tasks on its own, and anticipating a user's needs — such as ordering coffee.

Counterpoint on Nov. 19 forecast that in the retail industry, "edge AI" revenue will surge sixfold by 2032, with roughly half of revenue coming from retail robots that clean and check inventory — often with the help of smaller AI models that can run on locally embedded chips.

But more capable, on-device AI also requires more battery power, Honor's Bai pointed out.

Honor's X70 Android smartphone, which launched last summer, boasts a battery with 8,300 milliampere-hours for a total device weight of just under 200 grams. That compares with 5,000 mAH for the 214-gram flagship Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra, and roughly 5,400 mAH for this year's 249-gram Apple iPhone 18 Pro Max, according to GSM Arena.

LimX's Oli humanoid comes with 9,500 mAH battery capacity, lasting the robot about 1.5 hours.

Such AI applications, however, remain sensitive to the memory-chip shortage weighing on the industry.

The X70 was the best-selling Android phone in the entry to mid-tier range, said Shenzhen-based Counterpoint analyst Shiwen Ma.

While Ma said penetration of smartphones with generative AI features is higher in China at 55% versus 38% overseas, the offerings are still not essential to users, while rising memory-chip costs make it harder for manufacturers to bring AI-powered devices to the mass market.

Geely-backed automotive tech company EcarX's CFO Dylan Jeng told me earlier this month he expects the memory price surge to persist through 2027, and even the first half of 2028.

The chipmaker that can narrow the gap for on-device AI needs could find its moment soon.

Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke exclusively on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week.

How China is using summits to expand its global influence

For a second year in a row, Xi has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores. But U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returned from Asia.

Putin scores 'massive win' at first trilateral meeting

The first-ever three-way summit is "arguably a diplomatic victory for Moscow," said Andrius Tursa, an advisor for Central and Eastern Europe and Russia at Teneo. The view from inside the APEC welcome banquet showed the leaders were in close proximity from the start of the summit.

#2687. The Seahawks offense is not all Brian Fleury’s fault

The Seattle Seahawks bye week has come and gone, and now the Super Bowl champions must begin a sprint to the finish line to make the playoffs. While the defense has remained incredibly difficult to crack (except in the red zone, bizarrely), the offense has been inconsistent at best and self-destructive at its absolute worst. If the Seahawks fail to reach the postseason, the failures of the offense will likely be the main culprit.

First-year offensive coordinator Brian Fleury has come under heavy criticism for his unit's performance. Despite retaining most of last year's Super Bowl offense (with one very notable exception), the Seahawks have not performed nearly as well as they did under Klint Kubiak in 2025. It's certainly not inconceivable that Fleury is a one-and-done OC if things don't take a positive turn over the final seven weeks of the regular season.

And yet, as we have previously written, the cracks in Seattle's offense did not start with Fleury. And we have numbers to back it up.

A fantastic article by Ted Nguyen and Seahawks beat reporter Andrew Destin of The Athletic outlined the quantifiable decline in Seattle's passing game. The inflection point is the loss to the Los Angeles Rams, in which Sam Darnold was picked off four times and the offense didn't score a touchdown until late in the fourth quarter. It was a game that looked nowhere near the red-hot previous 10 weeks in which Sam Darnold looked like a darkhorse MVP candidate. The Rams might have lost subsequent games to the Seahawks, but they laid out the blueprint for things to come.

The good news is Seattle, believe it or not, is statistically faring better through 2026 than the back-end of 2025. It is nowhere close to the gaudiness of Weeks 1-10 of last year. You know what changed? The coverage. More specifically, the evidence is clear that Seahawks opponents are leveling up on their two-high shells. Again, the EPA/pass is better than how 2025 ended but not at the same level of Weeks 1-10 2025.

The article doesn't have the splits between Drew Lock and Sam Darnold when factoring in the passing stats, although I suspect Lock is carrying the bulk of the success by virtue of not turning the ball over. What is clear is that the passing offense, even with Jaxon Smith-Njigba performing like he's completely solved football, is not very explosive.

You know what can help replace some of the lack of explosives in the passing game? Running the ball better against two-high looks and lighter boxes. Seattle was not necessarily otherworldly efficient from Week 11 through the Super Bowl, but the explosives went up and the negative rushes went down. In 2026? Not explosive, not efficient, not good at all.

Such is life when Kenneth Walker walks and the only plan at running back was hoping Jadarian Price, Emanuel Wilson, and George Holani could hold down the fort until Zach Charbonnet came back. Price got injured, Wilson is a capable back but not an explosive threat, and Holani is a fine third-down option who has a single 10+ yard run to his name. Charbonnet and Price are thankfully healthy and have both had good moments against very bad defenses, and they will be relied upon heavily in the home stretch.

I don't think that Brian Fleury should be treated as the main culprit for an offense that was wobbling under Klint Kubiak. Does anyone here remember the zero touchdowns scored against the Philip Rivers-led Indianapolis Colts? Or the 6-6 halftime score against the Atlanta Falcons? Or going 3-3 at the half versus the Carolina Panthers? Or not scoring an offensive touchdown until garbage time against the Minnesota Vikings? That's what the Seahawks offense looked like for most of the end of last season. They literally won the Super Bowl scoring only one offensive touchdown while Sam Darnold could only complete half of his 38 pass attempts!

Fleury can take culpability, however, for the sloppiness of the operation. Seattle is on pace for more false starts than all of last year (playoffs included). Fleury is the one constantly calling first-and-goal run plays to mostly unsatisfying results. Ted Nguyen's article highlighted ongoing issues that ultimately fall at this feet:

The offense has also seen a lack of precision and detail, leading to poor spacing on pass concepts, shoddy blocking and turnovers. Taking care of the details is imperative to their improvement.

But perhaps another aspect of the offense's struggles is the harsh truth about Seattle's receiver room, which looked like a deep and talented group but isn't performing like one. Nguyen's assessment of Seattle's non-JSN targets stops short of being scathing but the stats seem telling:

Smith-Njigba is still one of the best receivers against man, but the rest of the team has struggled. Last season, every skill player other than Smith-Njigba averaged -0.04 EPA per target with an explosive rate of 11.9 percent. This season, they average -0.19 EPA per target with an explosive play rate of 7.8 percent.

The Seahawks do not have a second receiver who can reliably beat man coverage on his own. Shaheed is a gadget player who can win deep occasionally. Kupp has been efficient, but he's too slow to be a high-volume target against man. Horton flashed last season, but he's struggled to create separation. Barner can be a mismatch against some teams but hasn't had enough plays designed for him this season.

The Seahawks' auxiliary players are what they are, and they need help from the scheme to get open.

Fleury doesn't appear to be doing enough to counter man coverage by scheming guys open, which the Seahawks have struggled with all season, but if your receiving group outside of JSN constantly needs scheme help to be effective, you've got a bigger problem on your hands. I don't think it's far-fetched to say that Seattle's wide receiver group could and arguably should look very different next year given Kupp's sunsetting career and the disappointing start to Rashid Shaheed's new contract.

The Seahawks offense isn't functioning well in part because opposing defenses (well, good ones at least) have long figured out how to slow them down for the better part of 1.5 seasons. It was happening under Kubiak but the Seahawks kept winning anyway, and indeed they still had some brilliant playoff performances, more specifically the NFC Championship. Injuries, offensive line struggles, and having a poor run game for most of this season have exacerbated the problems, and they have certainly not helped Sam Darnold in any of his starts. Drew Lock has admittedly passed his tests in Darnold's absence, but it'd be very optimistic to assume that he'll continue playing well when he's not facing teams whose coordinators have since been fired.

The fact that Darnold's once gaudy numbers have dipped under two coordinators once teams started to increase two-high shells may also speak to an exposing of Darnold himself. Getting "greedy" cost Seattle a win against the Denver Broncos when Darnold was picked off forcing a bad ball to Shaheed against a Cover 2 look. If this is what nerfs Darnold consistently regardless of coordinator, then we've somehow managed a new version of the collapse of Let Russ Cook. Maybe that costs this Brian his job, too.

I'm not saying Brian Fleury is faultless nor am I going to push back on those who want him fired, but these problems did not just show up overnight, and Fleury hasn't exactly been put in favorable spots considering the season-long injury woes. Frankly, I think I've had my fill of several years worth of constantly blaming the coordinator for the shortcomings of the incumbent quarterback. The question is whether or not Fleury (and, frankly, anyone in Seattle's QB room) should be the one to solve these issues beyond 2026.

#2686. Jaylen Warren, Luther Burden II, and other Week 11 fantasy football bad beats not to worry about

Jaylen Warren, Luther Burden II, and other Week 11 fantasy football bad beats not to worry about appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

One quiet box score in November is enough to make any fantasy football manager hit the panic button, especially with the playoffs rapidly approaching. Yet chasing single-week results without examining underlying usage is one of the fastest ways to sabotage a championship contender. With six teams on bye thinning out lineups across ClutchPoints' Week 11 running back rankings and Week 11 wide receiver Start 'Em, Sit 'Em slate, several trusted starters delivered frustrating duds despite healthy roles. Before making any rash benchings or lopsided trades, take a deep breath on these four Week 11 bad beats.

Managers who plugged Warren into their lineups watched him muster just nine carries for 28 rushing yards and two catches for one receiving yard in Pittsburgh's 19-16 road loss to Philadelphia. Finishing with 29 total yards hurts, but game context softens the blow. Aaron Rodgers attempted 44 passes as Pittsburgh struggled to generate running room against a stout Eagles defensive front, capping every Steelers rusher. More importantly, Warren still led Rico Dowdle in carries and ran 36 routes compared to Dowdle's 16, proving his grip on passing-down snaps remains intact. After ripping off elite production from Weeks 3 through 8, Warren stays firmly in the RB2 mix entering Week 12.

Burden burned fantasy rosters during Chicago's 21-13 home victory over New Orleans, catching two of three targets for 10 yards while adding a three-yard carry. That 13-yard output looks alarming until you inspect the Bears' conservative game plan. Playing with a steady lead throughout the afternoon, Chicago leaned heavily on its ground attack while quarterback Caleb Williams threw only 23 passes, leaving no Bears pass-catcher with more than four targets. Burden still paces Chicago with 51 receptions, 654 receiving yards, and four touchdowns across 10 games. Expect a quick rebound when the Bears visit Detroit in a high-scoring Thanksgiving clash.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Two proven wideouts also posted misleading duds in Week 11. Returning from a two-month hamstring injury in Philadelphia's win over Pittsburgh, DeVonta Smith turned 12 targets into 41 receiving yards with Joe Milton under center. Commanding that level of immediate target volume signals huge upside once Smith shakes off the rust, much like the usage-driven plays highlighted in ClutchPoints' Week 11 tight end rankings and Week 11 quarterback Start 'Em, Sit 'Em breakdown.

Meanwhile, New York Jets star Garrett Wilson caught three of five targets for 39 yards in a 27-24 upset over the Los Angeles Chargers. Wilson generated strong yardage after the catch by breaking tackles on multiple receptions and only missed a touchdown because Geno Smith badly overthrew him near the end zone from the six-yard line. Sitting at 53 catches for 672 yards and three scores on the season, Wilson remains an automatic starter against Miami in Week 12.

#2685. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

Contracts ask traders if the S&P 500 will go above various levels in 2026, and they're resolved using data from S&P Dow Jones Indices.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The S&P 500 has slashed its year-to-date gain to just over 2%.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2684. Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, ‘miserable' weeks

Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, 'miserable' weeks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow is on the verge of his season debut with the Sixers.

The team listed Barlow as probable for its Saturday afternoon matchup with the Bucks at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Barlow practiced Friday and has been making clear progress in his recent on-court work recovering from an injury formally classified as a "lower back stress reaction."

He was a reliable rotation fixture last season, playing 71 regular-season games and starting 59. Barlow posted 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals per contest in 23.8 minutes per game. The Sixers then made a common-sense call to exercise his $3.4 million option for the 2026-27 season.

The 23-year-old described his back injury as "an overuse thing."

"I guess there was an area or maybe I started my (offseason) workouts too early," he said. "I don't know what it was fully in terms of what caused it. Once it happened, it happened. Nothing I can do about it besides try to correct it and get my body feeling as good as possible."

He called the past few weeks "miserable" as he rehabbed and waited on the green light by the Sixers' training staff.

"The days just felt longer," Barlow said. "Trying to be engaged mentally, which I think I've done a good job with, but the physical aspect wasn't there. I'm somebody that's prided himself on competing, being available. Not being able to do that was hard, but I'm excited to kind of put this past me."

With LeBron James and Jaylen Brown on the team now, Barlow won't start at forward when the Sixers are at full strength.

He's still a multi-dimensional frontcourt piece for Sixers head coach Nick Nurse who may very well get substantial opportunities at backup center.

"Just cutting, playing hard, being ready to make the open shot," Barlow said. "And then I don't think you can ever have too much defensive versatility. I think that's something I can bring, try to help us be more versatile."

When Barlow checks into his first game of the season, he'll appreciate the moment.

"You don't really realize what you have until it's not there (anymore)," he said. "I'm just excited to play. …. Just playing the first stint … that's going to be the main thing I'm looking forward to."

A Sixers official said the following players were all "unrestricted" at the team's light practice:

Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness)

Bona still needs to practice with contact before a return is on the table, according to the official. Saturday's game will mark his eighth consecutive absence.

Brown and Wade have both been ruled out for the 13-3 Sixers' meeting with the 1-15 Bucks. Maxey was listed as questionable Friday night.

#2683. Jim Cramer sees more upside for Nvidia stock with Jensen Huang back as CEO

Nvidia's remarkable stock comeback isn't done yet now that Jensen Huang has returned as CEO, CNBC's Jim Cramer said Monday.

"It's terrific to have this admittedly tarnished national treasure back leading Nvidia," the "Mad Money" host said. "It's kind of a miracle given the board's insistence that he would never get the CEO job again. But it's a miracle that saved the Nasdaq which, in turn, saved the market, at least for now."

Nvidia shares soared 31.5% on the news, fueling a broader rally in AI infrastructure stocks. The S&P 500 jumped 4.7%, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite surged 6.5%, with both indexes posting their best days since April 2025. Monday's rebound erased nearly all of Nvidia's decline following Huang's Nov. 12 resignation amid reports of multiple extramarital affairs, including one involving an alleged Chinese spy. Before the rally Monday, the stock had fallen in all trading sessions since his departure.

"Jensen's only one man, and Nvidia is only one company," he said. "But the truth is that Jensen is the linchpin that holds the AI solar system together, and Nvidia is the biggest beneficiary."

Cramer argued that Huang's reinstatement removes a major source of uncertainty for investors, strengthening the case for a higher valuation. The chipmaker delivered blockbuster quarterly results last week, raising its revenue growth outlook to 75%, but the results did little to stem the sell-off as investors remained concerned about its leadership.

"If it weren't for the Jensen overhang, and the insistence that Jensen could never come back, ... I think the stock would have been up significantly on that quarter," Cramer said.

Still, Cramer cautioned that Huang's return doesn't erase the broader headwinds facing Nvidia. Growing political opposition to data center construction following the midterm elections could slow AI infrastructure expansion, while uncertainty surrounding chip sales to China threatens a potential source of demand. Meanwhile, a more hawkish Federal Reserve has pressured technology stock valuations as investors brace for higher interest rates.

Those challenges, Cramer argued, make it difficult to justify a return to the $213 level Nvidia traded at before The New York Times first reported about Huang's relationship with a woman suspected of ties to Chinese intelligence on Oct. 29. But with Huang back at the helm and the company's growth outlook intact, he said the stock deserves a higher valuation. The stock currently trades at roughly 10 times earnings estimates for Nvidia's fiscal 2028, according to FactSet.

"There is just no way that, with the return of Jensen, this stock deserves the same 10 times price to earnings multiple," Cramer said.

"These things are subjective, of course, but to start, let's say this stock deserves to trade at twelve times next year's earnings estimate, which would make this a $199 stock," Cramer said. "That's my target."
